Arc coordinates for maximal representations
Marta Magnani

Abstract
. We generalize arc coordinates for maximal representations from a hyperbolic surface with boundary into PSp ⁡ ( 4 , ℝ ) {\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}) , focusing on the case where the surface is a pair of pants. We introduce geometric parameters within the space of right-angled hexagons in the Siegel space 𝒳 \mathcal{X} . These parameters enable the visualization of a right-angled hexagon as a polygonal chain inside the hyperbolic plane ℍ 2 \mathbb{H}^{2} . We explore the geometric properties of reflections in 𝒳 \mathcal{X} and introduce the notion of maximal representation of the reflection group W 3 = ℤ / 2 ​ ℤ ∗ ℤ / 2 ​ ℤ ∗ ℤ / 2 ​ ℤ W_{3}=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z} . We parametrize maximal representations from W 3 W_{3} into PSp ± ​ ( 4 , ℝ ) {\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}) , this induces a natural parametrization of a subset of maximal and Shilov hyperbolic representations into PSp ⁡ ( 4 , ℝ ) {\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}) .



1.
Introduction

1.1.
The space of maximal representations

Given
\Sigma
a closed oriented surface of negative Euler characteristic and fundamental group
\Gamma
, the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
is the parameter space of marked hyperbolic structures on
\Sigma
. It is well known that, with the introduction of the holonomy map, one can associate to a point in
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
a discrete and faithful representation
\rho:\Gamma\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
so that the surface
\Sigma
is realized by the quotient
\Sigma=\rho(\Gamma)\backslash\mathbb{H}^{2}
. This representation is well defined up to conjugation by an element in
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
so that the space
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
can be identified with a connected component of the representation variety
{\rm Hom}(\Gamma,{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}))/{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
which consists entirely of discrete and faithful representations
[
Gol80
]
.

This phenomenon of the representation variety to admit components consisting only of injective homomorphisms with discrete image is still true if we substitute
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with a semisimple real Lie group of higher rank
G
. In this sense higher rank Teichmüller space was developed as a generalization of classical Teichmüller space. More precisely given
G
a semisimple real Lie group of higher rank, a
higher Teichmüller space
is a subset of
{\rm Hom}(\Gamma,G)/G
which is a union of connected components that consist entirely of discrete and faithful representations. To such a representation
\rho
we can associate the quotient
\rho(\Gamma)\backslash\mathcal{X}
where
\mathcal{X}
is the symmetric space associated to
G
. The space
\mathcal{X}
is a non-positively curved Riemannian symmetric manifold of higher rank, where
rank
denotes the maximal dimension of an isometrically embedded flat inside
\mathcal{X}
. The quotient
\rho(\Gamma)\backslash\mathcal{X}
is a locally symmetric space whose fundamental group is isomorphic to the fundamental group of
\Sigma
.

There are two well-known families of higher Teichmüller spaces: Hitchin components and maximal representations. In this paper we are interested in maximal representations. These are defined when
G
is a Hermitian Lie group such as
{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
and are singled out by the maximal value of the Toledo number, which is a generalization of the Euler number. Burger Iozzi and Wienhard
[
BILW05
]
,
[
BIW10
]
studied the Toledo invariant for general Hermitian Lie groups and proved that maximal representations
{\rm Hom}^{\text{max}}(\Gamma,G)
can be characterised as those representations admitting a monotone equivariant boundary map
\xi:S^{1}\to\widecheck{S}
, where
\widecheck{S}
denotes the Shilov boundary and coincides with the set of real Lagrangians
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
when
G
is
{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
.

There is a related theory for surfaces with punctures or boundary components. The first thing to notice is that when
\partial\Sigma\neq\varnothing
then
\Gamma
is a free group and the whole representation variety is connected. Denote
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,m}
a surface of genus
g
and
m
boundary components with fundamental group
\Gamma_{g,m}
. Let

\Gamma_{g,m}=\langle a_{1},b_{1},...a_{g},b_{g},c_{1},...c_{m}|\ \prod_{i=1}^{g}[a_{i},b_{i}]\prod_{j=1}^{m}c_{j}=1\rangle

be a presentation where the elements
c_{i}
represent loops which are freely homotopic to the corresponding boundary components of
\partial\Sigma
with positive orientation. A boundary condition might be imposed by considering

(1)

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}^{\widecheck{S}}(\Gamma_{g,m},G)=\{\rho\in{\rm Hom}(\Gamma_{g,m},G)\,|\,\rho(c_{i})\text{ has at least}

\displaystyle\text{one fixed point in }\widecheck{S},\quad 1\leq i\leq m\}

where
\widecheck{S}
denotes the Shilov boundary. In this case
{\rm Hom}^{\text{max}}(\Gamma_{g,m},G)\subset{\rm Hom}^{\widecheck{S}}(\Gamma_{g,m},G)
and in particular
{\rm Hom}^{\text{max}}(\Gamma_{g,m},G)
is a union of connected components of the set
{\rm Hom}^{\widecheck{S}}(\Gamma_{g,m},G)

[
BIW10
, Corollary 14]
. In this paper we are interested in maximal representations inside (
1
) that satisfy a further condition: we will fix a union of conjugacy classes by imposing in (
1
) that every
\rho(c_{i})
fixes exactly two points in
\widecheck{S}
on which it acts expandingly and contractingly respectively. This is equivalent for the representation to be Anosov in the sense of
[
GW12
]
. We denote this space
{\rm Hom}^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{g,m},G)
.

1.2.
The results

Coordinates on the space of maximal representations often arise as a generalization of well known coordinates on the classical Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
. Analogues of Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates were developed by Strubel
[
Str15
]
, whereas analogues of shear coordinates were developed by Alessandrini Guichard Rogozinnikov and Wienhard
[
AGRW19
]
.

We want to generalize arc coordinates to the space of maximal representations. In classical Teichmüller theory arc coordinates were introduced by Harer
[
Har86
]
and developed by Penner
[
Pen87
]
to decompose decorated Teichmüller space of punctured surface. This decomposition was generalized by
[
Ush99
]

[
Pen02
]
for surfaces with boundary. Similar coordinates were used in
[
Luo07
]

[
Guo09
]
and in
[
Mon09
]
.

We will consider the case where
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,m}
is a compact orientable smooth surface of genus
g
and
m
boundary components. We denote
\Gamma_{g,m}
the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,m})
, which is isomorphic to the free group
\mathbb{F}_{2g+m-1}
. An element
\gamma\in\Gamma_{g,m}
is called
peripheral
if it is represented by a loop that is freely homotopic into a boundary component of
\Sigma_{g,m}
. We can equip
\Sigma_{g,m}
with a complete hyperbolic structure of finite volume with geodesic boundary. The universal covering
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{g,m}
of
\Sigma_{g,m}
is a closed subset of the hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}
where boundary curves are geodesics.

Arc coordinates are obtained by decomposing the surface in hexagons through the choice of a maximal collection
\{a_{1},...,a_{k}\}
of pairwise disjoint arcs with starting and ending point on a boundary component which are essential and pairwise non-homotopic. For every hexagon in this decomposition there are exactly three alternating edges belonging to
\partial\Sigma_{g,m}
. We denote by
E
the set of all edges and by
E_{bdry}
the set of edges lying on a boundary component. For a fixed hyperbolic structure we can always realize the hexagon decomposition of
\Sigma_{g,m}
in a way such that every edge is a geodesic and every arc is the unique geodesic which is orthogonal to the boundary at both endpoints. For each choice of
\{a_{1},...,a_{k}\}
we get a parametrization of the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma_{g,m})
: once we fix the lengths
l(a_{1}),...,l(a_{k})
there is a unique hyperbolic metric that makes
\Sigma_{g,m}\setminus\bigcup_{i}a_{i}
a union of hyperbolic right-angled hexagons where each hexagon has exactly three alternating edges
a_{i_{1}},a_{i_{2}},a_{i_{3}}
in
E\backslash E_{bdry}
of length
l(a_{i_{1}}),l(a_{i_{2}}),l(a_{i_{3}})
respectively, where
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\in\{1,...,k\}
. This is due to the
well known fact that given three real numbers
b,c,d>0
there exists (up to
isometries) a unique right-angled hexagon in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
with alternating sides of lengths

b,c
and
d
(see for example
[
Mar16
, Lemma 6.2.2]
). A point in the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma_{g,m})
is identified with a maximal representation
\rho:\Gamma_{g,m}\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Since we are considering surfaces with geodesic boundary, the image
\rho(\gamma)
of every element
\gamma\in\Gamma_{g,m}
is a hyperbolic isometry fixing exactly two points in
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
. The above discussion asserts that once we fix the lengths
l(a_{1}),...,l(a_{k})
we can explicitly write (up to conjugation) the maximal representation
\rho
such that
\Sigma_{g,m}=\rho(\Gamma_{g,m})\backslash\mathbb{H}^{2}
. An example for the surface
\Sigma_{0,3}
(pair of pants) is given in Figure
1
, where the fundamental group
\Gamma_{0,3}
is isomorphic to the free group
\mathbb{F}_{2}
generated by
\alpha
and
\beta
.

[formula]
[formula]
)
[formula]
)
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 1.
The maximal representation
\rho:\Gamma_{0,3}\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})

More generally, given a maximal representation
\rho:\Gamma_{g,m}\to{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, the image
\rho(\gamma)
of every non-peripheral element
\gamma\in\Gamma_{g,m}
is Shilov hyperbolic (see
[
Str15
]
). Equivalently,
\rho(\gamma)
fixes two transverse Lagrangians
l_{\gamma}^{+}
and
l_{\gamma}^{-}
on which it acts expandingly and contractingly respectively. These Lagrangians are the images
\xi(\gamma^{+})
and
\xi(\gamma^{-})
where
\xi:S^{1}\to\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
is the equivariant boundary map and
l^{\pm}_{\gamma}=\xi(\gamma^{\pm})
.
We consider the set of maximal representations where the property of being Shilov hyperbolic is true also for peripheral elements.

Definition
.

(see
7.5
)
A maximal representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
is called
Shilov hyperbolic
if
\rho(\gamma)
is Shilov hyperbolic for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. The set of maximal representations which are Shilov hyperbolic is denoted by
\text{Hom}^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R}))
. We define
\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R}))
as the quotient

\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})):=\text{Hom}^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R}))/_{{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})}

where
{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
is acting by conjugation:
\rho\sim\rho^{\prime}
if there exists
g\in{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
such that
\rho(\gamma)=g\rho^{\prime}(\gamma)g^{-1}
for all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.

We focus on the case where
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,3}
and
n=2
: we consider the Siegel space
\mathcal{X}
the symmetric space associated to
{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
and we fix the Weyl chamber
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}

\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|\ x_{1}\geq x_{2}\geq 0\}

We denote by
\mathfrak{a}
the set of regular vectors inside
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}

\mathfrak{a}=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|\ x_{1}>x_{2}>0\}

and we further denote by
\mathfrak{d}
the set

\mathfrak{d}=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|\ x_{1}=x_{2}\}

The first step is to introduce a parameter space for right-angled hexagons in
\mathcal{X}
. The subspaces of the Siegel space that play the role of geodesics in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
are called
\mathbb{R}
-
tubes
. In Section
4.1
we give the definition of a right-angled hexagon
H
in
\mathcal{X}
, which is determined by a cyclic sequence of
\mathbb{R}
-tubes

H=[\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{4},\mathcal{Y}_{5},\mathcal{Y}_{6}]

where any two consecutive tubes are orthogonal. We further define the set of
ordered right-angled hexagons

\mathcal{H}
, this is given by the data
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{1})
of a right-angled hexagon together with the choice of a tube
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
. We distinguish between generic (Definition
4.6
) and non-generic hexagons (Section
4.2
). A generic hexagon is parametrized by length parameters
\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d}
inside
\mathfrak{a}
and angle parameters
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
lying in
[0,2\pi)
(Proposition
4.15
). In the non-generic case some length parameters lie in
\mathfrak{d}
and some angle parameters vanish (Propositions
4.20
,
4.21
and
4.22
). This leads to a geometric visualization of a right-angled hexagon inside
\mathcal{X}
in terms of a polygonal chain (Section
4.4
). A parameter space which encloses both generic and non-generic hexagons is given by

Theorem
.

(see
4.25
)
The space
\mathcal{H}
is parametrized up to isometry by
\mathcal{A}=\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{3}\times[0,2\pi)^{2}/_{\sim}
.

where
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}=\mathfrak{a}\cup\mathfrak{d}
. The equivalence relation collapses one of the angles to a point in the case where the hexagon degenerates to a non-generic one. These parameters were firstly introduced with the aim of generalizing hexagon parameters in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. This approach turned out to be very tricky and this is explained in detail in Section
5
. Geometric parameters for a maximal representation
\rho:\Gamma_{0,3}\to{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
should be thought as the data of lengths and angles which uniquely determine two adjacent hexagons in
\mathcal{X}
both having three alternating sides of length
\underline{b},\underline{c}
and
\underline{d}
respectively. The maximal representation is then determined by the image of the generators of the fundamental group generalizing the geometric construction of Figure
1
. If we want to extend our hexagon-parameters for two adjacent hexagons by only changing the angle parameters we can not guarantee that the constructed hexagons have the same alternating side-lengths. We will therefore construct two adjacent hexagons starting with one hexagon
H
and obtaining the others by reflecting
H
across a side (Figure
2
).

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

The maximal representation
\rho:\Gamma_{0,3}\to{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})

This leads to the parametrization of a subset

\chi^{\mathcal{S}}\subset\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))

The idea is to see the fundamental group
\Gamma_{0,3}
as a subgroup of the reflection group

W_{3}=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}=\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}|\ s_{1}^{2}=s_{2}^{2}=s_{3}^{2}=1\rangle

through the following homomorphism
\phi

\displaystyle\phi:\Gamma_{0,3}

\displaystyle\to W_{3}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{1}s_{2}

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{2}s_{3}

We define the notion of maximal representation of the group
W_{3}
into
{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, where
{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(2n,\mathbb{R})
denotes the union of symplectic and antisymplectic matrices (Definition
6.8
).

Definition
.

(see
7.7
)
A representation
\rho:W_{3}\to{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(2n,\mathbb{R})
is
maximal
if there exists a maximal 6-tuple of Lagrangians
(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
such that
\rho(s_{1}),\rho(s_{2}),\rho(s_{3})
are reflections of
\mathcal{X}
fixing
(P_{1},P_{2}),(Q_{1},Q_{2}),(R_{1},R_{2})
respectively and such that

\begin{cases}\rho(s_{1})(X_{1})=X_{2}\text{ and }\rho(s_{1})(Z_{1})=Z_{2}\\
\rho(s_{2})(X_{1})=X_{2}\text{ and }\rho(s_{2})(Y_{1})=Y_{2}\\
\rho(s_{3})(Y_{1})=Y_{2}\text{ and }\rho(s_{3})(Z_{1})=Z_{2}\end{cases}

where
X_{1},X_{2},Y_{1},Y_{2},Z_{1},Z_{2}
are uniquely determined by (see Figure
3
)

\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{X_{1},X_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Q_{1},Q_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Y_{1},Y_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{R_{1},R_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Z_{1},Z_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}}

Theorem
.

(see
7.16
)
The set
\chi^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}))={\rm Hom}^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}))/{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
is parametrized by
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{K}^{3}
where
\mathcal{A}
is the parameter space of a right-angled hexagon and
\mathcal{K}
is the set

\mathcal{K}=\Big\{\begin{pmatrix}-K&0\\
0&K\end{pmatrix},\ K\in{\rm PO}(2),\ K^{2}={\rm Id}\Big\}

In Section
6.4
we give a geometrical interpretation of the set
\mathcal{K}
in terms of the polygonal chain associated to a right-angled hexagon. We prove that the restriction to
\Gamma_{0,3}
of such a maximal representation is maximal and Shilov hyperbolic.

Proposition
.

(see
7.14
)
Fix
\widetilde{\rho}\in{\rm Hom}^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}))
.
Then the representation
\rho:=\widetilde{\rho}|_{Im(\phi)}
is inside
{\rm Hom}^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
.

This allows us to define
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
as the image
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}:=Im(f)
where
f
is the map

\displaystyle f:\chi^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}))

\displaystyle\to\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))

\displaystyle\bigl[\widetilde{\rho}\bigr]

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl[\widetilde{\rho}|_{Im(\phi)}\bigr]

The parametrization of
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
is obtained by imposing an equivalent relation on
\mathcal{S}
which identifies the points that have same image under
f
.

Theorem
.

(see
7.18
)
The set
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
is parametrized by
\mathcal{S}/_{\sim}

In Corollary
7.19
we show that, contrary to the hyperbolic case (Proposition
7.12
), the map
f
is not injective nor surjective. The set
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
is an 8-dimensional subspace inside the 10-dimensional space
\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
. The space
\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
has 4 connected components (see
[
AGRW19
]
), and the paramater space for
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
gives 8 connected components. In Proposition
7.21
we show that
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
hits all connected components of
\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
.

A motivation for this work is to study compactification of character varieties where similar arguments can be carried out with non-Archimedean Siegel spaces as in
[
BP17
]
. We expect applications of this work in the study of the real spectrum compactification of maximal character varieties (see
[
BIPP21
]
,
[
BIPP23
]
) Of particular interest are rank two groups where
[
BIPP21a
]
and
[
OT23a
]

[
OT23
]
suggest a link with flat structures with angle multiple of
\frac{\pi}{2}
. Developing arc coordinates for those would be interesting.

1.3.
Organization of the paper

Section
2
recalls some fundamental properties of the Siegel space
\mathcal{X}
- the symmetric space associated to
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
. We fix notation and focus on the case of
{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
. In Section
3
we define the set of generic quintuples and give a parameter space for them (Proposition
3.7
). These parameters will be very useful for the parametrization of right-angled hexagons. Section
4
is dedicated to the study of hexagons. We define the set of ordered right-angled hexagons (Definition
4.3
) and distinguish between generic (Definition
4.6
) and non-generic hexagons (Section
4.2
). We introduce a parameter space for both cases (Proposition
4.15
for the generic case and Propositions
4.20
,
4.21
and
4.22
for the non-generic case). A parameter space which encloses both generic and non-generic hexagons is given in Theorem
4.25
. These parameters will be called
arc coordinates
. In Section
5
we show how arc coordinates arise from the idea of generalizing coordinates of a hexagon in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and explain the problems encountered in this approach. In Section
6
we discuss properties of reflections in the Siegel space
\mathcal{X}
. We define the
reflection set associated to the side of a hexagon
(Definition
6.18
) and give a geometric interpretation of it (Section
6.4
). In Section
7
we define the set
\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R}))
and we further define the notion of a maximal representation from the group
W_{3}=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
into
{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(2n,\mathbb{R})
(Definition
7.7
), providing a parameter space for the
{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R})
-case (Theorem
7.16
). We further define the set
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}\subset\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
(Definition
7.15
) to which we provide a parameter space in Theorem
7.18
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
The Siegel space

The
Siegel space

\mathcal{X}
is the symmetric space associated to the symplectic group
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
. Recall that the symplectic group

{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})=\{M\in{\rm SL}(2n,\mathbb{R})\ |\ M^{T}J_{n}M=J_{n}\}

is the subgroup of
{\rm SL}(2n,\mathbb{R})
preserving the symplectic form
\omega(\cdot,\cdot)
represented, with respect to the standard basis, by the matrix

J_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}0&{\rm Id}_{n}\\
-{\rm Id}_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}

The group
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
can also be described as the group of block matrices:

{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})=\Big\{\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}|\ A^{T}C,B^{T}D\text{ symmetric, and }A^{T}D-C^{T}B={\rm Id}_{n}\Big\}

When
n=1
the group
{\rm Sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
coincides with
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. There are two models commonly used for the Siegel space: the
upper-half space
and the
Borel embedding
model.

The
upper-half space model
is a generalization of the upper-half space model of the hyperbolic plane and is given by a specific set of symmetric matrices:

\mathcal{X}=\{X+iY,\ X\in{\rm Sym}(n,\mathbb{R}),\ Y\in{\rm Sym}^{+}(n,\mathbb{R})\}

where
{\rm Sym}(n,\mathbb{R})
denotes the set of
n
-dimensional symmetric matrices with coefficients in
\mathbb{R}
and
{\rm Sym}^{+}(n,\mathbb{R})
is the subset of
{\rm Sym}(n,\mathbb{R})
given by positive definite matrices. The group
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
acts on
\mathcal{X}
by fractional linear transformations:

\centering\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}\cdot Z=(AZ+B)(CZ+D)^{-1}\@add@centering

The
Borel embedding model
is given by

\mathbb{X}=\{l\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2n})|\ \ i\omega(\sigma(\cdot),\cdot)_{\mathbb{C}}|_{l\times l}\ \text{is positive definite}\}

where
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2n})
is the set of Lagrangians and
\sigma:\mathbb{C}^{2n}\to\mathbb{C}^{2n}
denotes complex conjugation. We consider the affine chart
\iota:{\rm Sym}(n,\mathbb{C})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2n})
that associates to a symmetric matrix
Z
the linear subspace of
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
spanned by the columns of the matrix
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}Z\\
{\rm Id}_{n}\\
\end{array}\right)_{2n\times n}
. This induces an
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
-equivariant identification
\iota:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{X}
. The restriction of the affine chart
\iota
to the subspace
{\rm Sym}(n,\mathbb{R})
provides a parametrization of the set of real Lagrangians that are transverse as linear subspaces to
\langle e_{1},...,e_{n}\rangle
, which will be denoted by
l_{\infty}
or just
\infty
.

2.2.
Boundary and Lagrangians

The set of real Lagrangians
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
naturally arises as the unique closed
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
-orbit in the boundary of
\mathcal{X}
in its Borel embedding and for
this reason
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
is the Shilov boundary of the bounded domain realization of
\mathcal{X}
(see
[
Wie04
]
). Denote by
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{(k)}
the set of
k
-tuples of pairwise transverse Lagrangians. It is easy to prove that the group
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
acts transitively on
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{(2)}
. Moreover, it has
(n+1)
orbits in
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{(3)}
, indexed by the Maslov index (see for example
[
Sou05
]
). The Maslov index is cyclically invariant, is invariant under the action of
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
on
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{(3)}
and the group
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
acts transitively on the set of triples of pairwise transverse Lagrangians with the same Maslov index
[
LV80
]
. The value of the Maslov index is maximal on the orbit of
(\langle e_{1},...,e_{n}\rangle,\langle e_{n+1},...,e_{2n}\rangle,\langle e_{1}+e_{n+1},...,e_{n}+e_{2n}\rangle)=(l_{\infty},0,{\rm Id})
.

Definition 2.1
.

A triple of pairwise transverse Lagrangians is called
maximal
if it is in the
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
-orbit of
(l_{\infty},0,{\rm Id})
. An m-tuple
(l_{1},...,l_{m})
is
maximal
if for every
i<j<k
the triple
(l_{i},l_{j},l_{k})
is maximal.

Maximal triples are a generalization of positively oriented triples in the circle
S^{1}
and they play a central role in the study of maximal representations. It is useful to have a concrete criterion to check when triples of Lagrangian are maximal.

Lemma 2.2
.

(
[
BP17
]
)
The following hold:

(1)
Any cyclic permutation of a maximal triple is maximal;

(2)
The triple
(l_{\infty},X,Y)
is maximal if and only if
Y-X
is positive definite;

(3)
If
Z-X
is positive definite, the triple
(X,Y,Z)
is maximal if and only if
Z-Y
and
Y-X
are positive definite.

From a given maximal
m
-tuple we can obtain a maximal
(m+k)
-tuple by adding a maximal
k
-tuple between two consecutive Lagrangians.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
(P_{1},...,P_{m})
be a maximal
m
-tuple. For
i\in\{1,...,m-1\}
and
k\geq 1
let
(P_{i},Q_{1},...,Q_{k},P_{i+1})
be maximal. Then the
(m+k)
-tuple
(P_{1},...,P_{i},Q_{1},...,Q_{k},P_{i+1},...,P_{m})
is maximal.

Proof.

Up to isometry we reduce to the case where
P_{1}=0,P_{m}=l_{\infty}
, that is we consider the
(m+k)
-tuple
(0,P_{2},...P_{i},Q_{1},...,Q_{k},P_{i+1},...,l_{\infty})
where
(0,P_{2},...,P_{m-1},l_{\infty})
maximal and
(P_{i},Q_{1},...,Q_{k},P_{i+1})
maximal. Using Lemma
2.2
result follows immediately.
∎

2.3.
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
-invariant distance

We introduce a
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
-invariant distance on the symmetric space
\mathcal{X}
. Fix a point
p
in a maximal flat
F
and a Weyl chamber
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}\subset T_{p}F
. This is a fundamental domain for the action of
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
on the tangent bundle
T\mathcal{X}
. In our case we have

\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}=\{(x_{1},...,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}|\ x_{1}\geq...\geq x_{n}\geq 0\}

A vector in the Weyl chamber is
regular
if all the inequalities are strict, which is equivalent to being contained in a unique flat. In order to define a vectorial
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
-invariant distance in
\mathcal{X}
we need to recall from
[
BP17
]
the definition of an endomorphism-valued cross-ratio. If
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
are transverse (denoted by
l_{1}\pitchfork l_{2}
), we denote by
p_{l_{1}}^{||l_{2}}:\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to l_{1}
the projection to
l_{1}
parallel to
l_{2}
.

Definition 2.4
.

For Lagrangians
l_{1},...,l_{4}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2n})
such that

l_{1}\pitchfork l_{2}
and
l_{3}\pitchfork l_{4}
the
cross-ratio

R(l_{1},l_{2},l_{3},l_{4})
is given in the Borel embedding model by the endomorphism of
l_{1}

R(l_{1},l_{2},l_{3},l_{4})=p_{l_{1}}^{||l_{2}}\circ p_{l_{4}}^{||l_{3}}|_{l_{1}}

In the upper half space model the explicit expression for the cross-ratio is given by (
[
BP17
, Lemma 4.2]
):

R(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4})=(X_{1}-X_{2})^{-1}(X_{4}-X_{2})(X_{4}-X_{3})^{-1}(X_{1}-X_{3})

where
R
is expressed with respect to the basis of
X_{1}
given by the columns of the matrix
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X_{1}\\
{\rm Id}_{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
.

Lemma 2.5

(
[
BP17
]
)
.

Assume
0,Z,X,l_{\infty}
are pairwise transverse. Then

R(0,Z,X,l_{\infty})=Z^{-1}X

We can now define the vectorial distance
d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}
. The fact that the cross-ratio can be used to describe the projection of a pair of points in
\mathcal{X}
onto the Weyl chamber was proved by Siegel in
[
Sie43
]
.

Definition 2.6
.

The
vectorial distance

d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}
is the projection onto the Weyl chamber
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{2}

\displaystyle\to\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}

\displaystyle(X,Z)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\log(\lambda_{1}),...,\log(\lambda_{n}))

where
\lambda_{i}=\frac{1+\sqrt{r_{i}}}{1-\sqrt{r_{i}}}
and
1>r_{1}\geq...\geq r_{n}\geq 0
are the eigenvalues of
R(X,\overline{Z},Z,\overline{X})

A pair of points in
\mathcal{X}
can be mapped to any other pair of points if and only if their vectorial distance
d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}
is the same. For interesting properties about the distance
d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}
see
[
Par10
]
,
[
KLP17
]
.

Lemma 2.7

(
[
FP20
]
)
.

Let
A
and
B
be positive definite symmetric matrices such that the difference
B-A
is positive definite. Let
\mu_{1}\geq...\geq\mu_{n}
be the eigenvalues of
A^{-1}B
. Then

d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}(iA,iB)=(\log\mu_{1},...,\log\mu_{n})

2.4.
Copies of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
inside the Siegel space
\mathcal{X}

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be the symmetric space associated to
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
. A
maximal polydisk
in
\mathcal{X}
is the image of a totally geodesic and holomorphic embedding of the Cartesian product of
n
copies of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
into
\mathcal{X}
.

We will focus on the symmetric space
\mathcal{X}
associated to
{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
. In this case an example of a maximal polydisk is the image of the following map
\psi
:

\displaystyle\psi:\mathbb{H}^{2}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}&0\\
0&z_{2}\end{pmatrix}

We will refer to this polydisk as the
model polydisk
since every other polydisk is translate of our model polydisk by an element in
{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
(see
[
Wol72
]
). Let
(M_{1},M_{2})
be an element of
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then
(M_{1},M_{2})
acts on the model polydisk as following:

(M_{1},M_{2})\cdot\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}&0\\
0&z_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}M_{1}(z_{1})&0\\
0&M_{2}(z_{2})\end{pmatrix}

where
M(z)
is the action on a point
z\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
by Möbius transformation. Let
\Delta
be the diagonal embedding given by

\displaystyle\Delta:{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\to{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\Big(\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&b_{1}\\
c_{1}&d_{1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}a_{2}&b_{2}\\
c_{2}&d_{2}\end{pmatrix}\Big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&0&b_{1}&0\\
0&a_{2}&0&b_{2}\\
c_{1}&0&d_{1}&0\\
0&c_{2}&0&d_{2}\end{pmatrix}

then
\psi(M_{1}(z_{1}),M_{2}(z_{2}))=\Delta(M_{1},M_{2})\big(\psi(z_{1},z_{2})\big)
. In particular the set
\psi\big((z,z)\big)
is a copy of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
inside
\mathcal{X}
and will be called the
diagonal disc
.

2.5.
\mathbb{R}
-tubes

The subspaces of the Siegel space that play the role of geodesics in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
are called
\mathbb{R}
-
tubes
. Let
\{a,b\}
be an unordered pair of transverse Lagrangians.

Definition 2.9
.

(
\mathbb{R}
-
tube
) The
\mathbb{R}
-
tube
associated to
\{a,b\}
is the set

\mathcal{Y}_{a,b}=\{l\in\mathcal{X}|\ R(a,l,\sigma(l),b)=-{\rm Id}\}

It can be proven (see
[
BP17
]
) that
\mathcal{Y}_{a,b}
is a totally geodesic subspace of
\mathcal{X}
of
the same real rank as
\mathcal{X}
and that it is the parallel set of the Riemannian singular geodesics whose endpoints in the visual boundary of
\mathcal{X}
are the Lagrangians
a
and
b
.
The group
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
acts transitively on
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{(2)}
and for every
g\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
it holds
g\cdot\mathcal{Y}_{a,b}=\mathcal{Y}_{ga,gb}
. Up to the symplectic group action we can therefore reduce to a model
\mathbb{R}
-tube, the one with endpoints
0
and
l_{\infty}
. In the upper-half space model this will be called the
standard tube
and consists of matrices of the form

\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}=\{iY|Y\in{\rm Sym}^{+}(n,\mathbb{R})\}

Intersection patterns of
\mathbb{R}
-tubes in the Siegel space reflect the intersection patterns of geodesics in the hyperbolic plane.

Proposition 2.10

(
[
FP20
]
)
.

If
(l_{1},l_{2},l_{3},l_{4})
is maximal, the intersection
\mathcal{Y}_{l_{1},l_{3}}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{l_{2},l_{4}}
consists of a single point and
\mathcal{Y}_{l_{1},l_{2}}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{l_{3},l_{4}}
is empty.

Definition 2.11
.

Two
\mathbb{R}
-tubes
\mathcal{Y}_{a,b}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{c,d}
are
orthogonal
if they are orthogonal as submanifolds of the symmetric space (where there is a well defined
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
-invariant scalar product).

Remark 2.12
.

The orthogonality relation can be expressed as a property of the cross-ratio of the boundary points:
if
(a,c,b,d)
is maximal, the
\mathbb{R}
-tubes
\mathcal{Y}_{a,b}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{c,d}
are orthogonal if and only if
R(a,c,b,d)=2{\rm Id}
(see
[
BP17
, Definition 4.14]
).

Denote by
((a,b)):=\{l\in\mathcal{L}|\ (a,l,b)\text{ is maximal}\}
and by

p_{a,b}:\mathcal{X}\cup((a,b))\to\mathcal{Y}_{a,b}

the orthogonal projection. It will be useful to have concrete expressions for the orthogonal projection to
((a,b))
when
(a,b)=(0,l_{\infty})
.

Lemma 2.13

(
[
FP20
]
)
.

For any
A\in{\rm Sym}^{+}(n,\mathbb{R})
the
\mathbb{R}
-tubes
\mathcal{Y}_{A,-A}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
are orthogonal and their unique intersection point is
iA
. In particular
p_{0,\infty}(A)=iA
.

Lemma 2.14

(
[
FP20
]
)
.

If
(a,x,y,b)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{4}
is a maximal 4-tuple and
p_{a,b}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{Y}_{a,b}
, the distance

d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}(p_{a,b}(x),p_{a,b}(y))=(\log\mu_{1},...,\log\mu_{n})

where
\mu_{i}
are the eigenvalues of the cross-ratio
R(a,x,y,b)
.

2.6.
Computing orthogonal tubes

A crucial tool to construct right-angled hexagons in the Siegel space
\mathcal{X}
is computing orthogonal
\mathbb{R}
-tubes. For this reason, this section lists concrete criteria to determine them.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4})
be a maximal
4
-tuple. Then there exists a unique tube
\mathcal{Y}_{P_{5},P_{6}}
orthogonal to both
\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{4}}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{P_{2},P_{3}}
.

Proof.

Up to
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
-action we can consider
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4})=(0,{\rm Id},P,\infty)
. By Lemma
2.13
the tubes orthogonal to
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
are of the form
Y_{-Q,Q}
where
Q\in{\rm Sym}^{+}(n,\mathbb{R})
. We want to find
Q
such that the triple
({\rm Id},Q,P)
is maximal and such that
\mathcal{Y}_{-Q,Q}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{{\rm Id},P}
. By the orthogonality condition (see Remark
2.12
) this happens if and only if
R(P,-Q,{\rm Id},Q)=2{\rm Id}
. Developing the left-hand side we obtain:

2(P+Q)^{-1}Q(Q-{\rm Id})^{-1}(P-{\rm Id})=2(P+Q)^{-1}\big((Q-{\rm Id})Q^{-1}\big)^{-1}(P-{\rm Id})=

=2\big(({\rm Id}-Q^{-1})(P+Q)\big)^{-1}(P-{\rm Id})=2{\rm Id}

This simplifies to
P+Q-Q^{-1}P-{\rm Id}=P-{\rm Id}
. We obtain
Q^{2}=P
which has unique solution
Q=\sqrt{P}
. In particular
P\in{\rm Sym}^{+}(n,\mathbb{R})
as
(0,{\rm Id},P,\infty)
is maximal (see Lemma
2.2
).
∎

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
(0,{\rm Id},P,\infty)
be a maximal quadruple. Then
\mathcal{Y}_{-\sqrt{P},\sqrt{P}}
is the unique
\mathbb{R}
-tube orthogonal to both
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{{\rm Id},P}
.

Proof.

Follows directly from the proof of Lemma
2.15
.
∎

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4})
be a maximal quadruple. Then the unique tube orthogonal to both
\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{4}}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{P_{2},P_{3}}
is
\mathcal{Y}_{Z_{1},Z_{2}}
where

Z_{1}=g^{-1}\big(-\sqrt{gP_{3}}\big),\ Z_{2}=g^{-1}\big(\sqrt{gP_{3}}\big)

g=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&A^{-T}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\rm Id}&(P_{1}-P_{4})^{-1}\\
0&{\rm Id}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&{\rm Id}\\
-{\rm Id}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\rm Id}&-P_{4}\\
0&{\rm Id}\end{pmatrix}

and

A=\sqrt{(P_{1}-P_{4})(P_{2}-P_{1})^{-1}(P_{2}-P_{4})}
.

Proof.

The matrix
g\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
is an isometry such that
(gP_{1},gP_{2},gP_{4})=(0,{\rm Id},\infty)
. Result follows from Lemma
2.16
.
∎

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4},P_{5},P_{6})
be a maximal 6-tuple and let
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},Q_{4}
be such that

\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Q_{1},Q_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{P_{3},P_{4}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Q_{3},Q_{4}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{P_{5},P_{6}}
. Then the quadruple
(P_{3},Q_{2},Q_{3},P_{4})
is maximal.

Proof.

Let
g\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
be such that
(gQ_{1},gQ_{2})=(\infty,0)
and
(gP_{3},gP_{4})=(-{\rm Id},{\rm Id})
.
We obtain the tubes
g\cdot\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}}=\mathcal{Y}_{-M,M}
and
g\cdot\mathcal{Y}_{P_{5},P_{6}}=\mathcal{Y}_{P,Q}
for some
M,P,Q
positive definite matrices. The tube
g\cdot\mathcal{Y}_{Q_{3},Q_{4}}=\mathcal{Y}_{X,Y}
is such that

(2)

\mathcal{Y}_{-{\rm Id},{\rm Id}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{X,Y}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{P,Q}

where
P
and
Q
are positive definite matrices. By construction of orthogonal tubes we know
(-{\rm Id},0,{\rm Id})
and
(X,{\rm Id},Y)
maximal (Lemma
2.16
). It is not hard to show that the matrix
X
needs to be positive definite for the condition (
2
) to be satisfied. It follows
(-{\rm Id},0,X,{\rm Id})
maximal and so is its preimage
(P_{3},Q_{2},Q_{3},P_{4})
.
∎

We end this section by giving some concrete expressions to find two orthogonal tubes when one of them is of the form
\mathcal{Y}_{-P,P}
for a positive definite matrix
P
. This configuration will turn out to be very useful when defining the parameter space of right-angled hexagons.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
(0,P_{1},P_{2},\infty)
be a maximal quadruple. Then

\mathcal{Y}_{-P_{1},P_{1}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1}P_{2}^{-1}P_{1},P_{2}}\text{ and }\ \mathcal{Y}_{-P_{2},P_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}P_{1}^{-1}P_{2}}

Proof.

For the first case it is sufficient to find
X\in{\rm Sym}(n,\mathbb{R})
such that

R(X,P_{1},P_{2},-P_{1})=2{\rm Id}
(see Remark
2.12
). Developing the left-hand side we obtain

(X-P_{1})^{-1}(-2P_{1})(-P_{1}-P_{2})^{-1}(X-P_{2})=2{\rm Id}

which can be rewritten
P_{1}(P_{1}+P_{2})^{-1}(X-P_{2})=(X-P_{1})
. This simplifies to

(X-P_{2})=(P_{1}+P_{2})P_{1}^{-1}(X-P_{1})=({\rm Id}+P_{2}P_{1}^{-1})(X-P_{1})=X-P_{1}+P_{2}P_{1}^{-1}X-P_{2}

We obtain
P_{2}P_{1}^{-1}X=P_{1}
. Result follows. The proof for the second case is the same.
∎

2.7.
The symmetric spaces
\mathcal{X}_{{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{R})}
and
\mathcal{X}_{{\rm SL}(n,\mathbb{R})}

Recall that the standard model for the symmetric space associated to
{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
is

\mathcal{X}_{{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{R})}={\rm Sym}^{+}(n,\mathbb{R})

We endow
\mathcal{X}_{{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{R})}
with the distance
d_{{\rm GL}}
given by

d_{{\rm GL}}(X,Y)=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\log\lambda_{i})^{2}}

where
\lambda_{i}
are the eigenvalues of
XY^{-1}
. With this choice of
d_{{\rm GL}}
the natural identification
\mathcal{X}_{{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{R})}=\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
is an isometry (where
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
is equipped with the induced Riemannian metric). Recall also that the symmetric space associated to
{\rm SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
is

\mathcal{X}_{{\rm SL}(n,\mathbb{R})}=\{X\in{\rm Sym}^{+}(n,\mathbb{R})|\ \det(X)=1\}

Similarly, we endow
\mathcal{X}_{{\rm SL}(n,\mathbb{R})}
with the distance
d_{{\rm SL}}
given by

d_{{\rm SL}}(X,Y)=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\log\lambda_{i})^{2}}

where
\lambda_{i}
are the eigenvalues of
XY^{-1}
.
In particular, the symmetric space
\mathcal{X}_{{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
can be identified with the hyperbolic upper-half plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}=\{z=x+iy|\ x,y\in\mathbb{R},\ y>0\}
via the following map:

\displaystyle h:\mathcal{X}_{{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{H}^{2}

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\mapsto\big[B\big]\cdot i

where
B
is an element of
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}
) and acts on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
via Möbius transformations. The inverse of
h
is given by
h^{-1}(z)=\sqrt{AA^{T}}
where
A\in{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and
A\cdot i=z
.

Remark 2.20
.

If we endow
\mathbb{H}^{2}
with the distance
d_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
relative to the standard metric
\frac{dx^{2}+dy^{2}}{y^{2}}
on the upper-half plane then
h
is not an isometry and in general
(\mathcal{X}_{{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})},d_{{\rm SL}})
and
(\mathbb{H}^{2},d_{\mathbb{H}^{2}})
are not isometric. To have an isometry we have to scale
d_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
by a factor of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
.

2.8.
The geometry of the standard tube
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}

Let us consider the symmetric space
\mathcal{X}
associated to
{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 2.21
.

The tube
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
is isometrically identified with
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

Proof.

As seen in Section
2.7
there is a natural identification
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}=\mathcal{X}_{{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
, where
\mathcal{X}_{{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
is the set of positive definite symmetric matrices. The map

\displaystyle f=\pi^{\mathbb{R}}\times\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}:\mathcal{X}_{{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}_{{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle\mapsto\Big(\frac{\log\det Q}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{Q}{\sqrt{\det Q}}\Big)

is a bijection, with inverse
f^{-1}\big((r,B)\big)=\sqrt{e^{\sqrt{2}r}}B
. When
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}_{{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
and
\mathcal{X}_{{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
are considered as metric spaces (endowed with
d_{\mathbb{R}}\times d_{{\rm SL}}
and
d_{{\rm GL}}
respectively), the map
f
is an isometry (
[
FP20
]
Lemma 2.17). The identification
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
follows from
\mathcal{X}_{{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}=\mathbb{H}^{2}
(see Section
2.7
). In particular all copies of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
in
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
are canonically identified. Observe that to turn
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
into an isometric identification we have to scale the metric
d_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
by a factor of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
(Remark
2.20
).
∎

Definition 2.22
.

Given
A\in{\rm Sym}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
the
hyperbolic component
of
A
is the point
\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(p_{0,\infty}(A))
. Similarly the
\mathbb{R}
-component of
A
is the point
\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(p_{0,\infty}(A))
and will be called
level
of
A
.

Let
\mathbb{H}^{2}=\{x+iy|\ x,y\in\mathbb{R},\ y>0\}
, then for any fixed level in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
the set of diagonal matrices coincides with the
y
-axis of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, where the set
\big\{\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix},\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2},\ \lambda_{1}\cdot\lambda_{2}=1\big\}
consists of points "above"
i\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
in the vertical
y
-axis of the hyperbolic plane.

Remark 2.23
.

Let
iX,iY\in\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
and
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}
such that
d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}(iX,iY)=(d_{1},d_{2})
. We can associate to
(d_{1},d_{2})
a more meaningful vector
(\mathbf{r}_{(d_{1},d_{2})},\mathbf{h}_{(d_{1},d_{2})})
(Figure
4
). The vector
(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{h})
based at
iX
has first coordinate
\mathbf{r}
equal to the difference between the levels of
iX
and
iY

\mathbf{r}=d^{\mathbb{R}}(\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(iX),\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(iY))

and second coordinate
\mathbf{h}
equal to the distance between the two points in
\mathbb{H}^{2}

\mathbf{h}=d^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iX),\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iY))

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 4.
Geometric interpretation of
(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{h})
for two points
iX,iY
in
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}

It is not hard to show that given
d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}(iX,iY)=(d_{1},d_{2})
then

\mathbf{r}=\frac{d_{1}+d_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}
and
\mathbf{h}=(d_{1}-d_{2})
. The vector
(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{h})
also gives a geometric condition for the maximality of the triple
(l_{\infty},X,Y)
i.e. for the matrix
Y-X
to be positive definite (see Lemma
2.2
). It holds (
[
FP20
, Corollary 2.21]
):

Y-X\text{ positive definite }\iff\mathbf{r}>\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbf{h}

The following Lemma of linear algebra will play a crucial role in the definition of parameters for a hexagon inside
\mathcal{X}
.

Lemma 2.24
.

For any
M\in{\rm Sym}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
with distinct eigenvalues there exist unique
S,Q
in
{\rm PSO}(2)
and
{\rm PO}(2)\backslash{\rm PSO}(2)
respectively such that

SMS^{T}=QMQ^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix},\text{where }\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}

Proof.

Let
v_{1},v_{2}
be orthonormal eigenvectors relative to the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>0
respectively and let
L
denote the orthogonal matrix
L=\Big(\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}v_{2}\end{bmatrix}\Big)
. If
\det L=1
, it is a standard fact of linear algebra that
S=\begin{pmatrix}[\ \ v_{1}^{T}\ \ ]\\
[\ \ v_{2}^{T}\ \ ]\end{pmatrix}=L^{T}
is the unique element of
{\rm PSO}(2)
such that
SMS^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix},\ \lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
. Put
Q=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}S=\begin{pmatrix}-[\ \ v_{1}^{T}\ \ ]\\
\ \ [\ \ v_{2}^{T}\ \ ]\end{pmatrix}
. Then
\det Q=-1
and
Q
is the desired matrix in
{\rm PO}(2)\backslash{\rm PSO}(2)
. If
\det L=-1
then the two diagonalizing matrices are
Q=L^{T}
and
S=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}Q
.

∎

2.9.
Geometric interpretation of diagonalization matrix

The group
{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
acts on a point
iM\in\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
via fractional linear transformations. In particular the isometries stabilizing the standard tube are of the form
\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&A^{-T}\end{pmatrix}
where
A\in{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Recall that we identify
{\rm Sym}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
(see Sections
2.7
and
2.8
). In this identification the identity matrix is identified with the point
(0,i)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Moreover, all copies of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
in
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
are canonically identified.
For a matrix
S\in{\rm PSO}(2)
we want to interpret the action

(3)

{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})\ni\begin{pmatrix}S&0\\
0&S\end{pmatrix}\cdot(iM)=iSMS^{T}

as a transformation which fixes the level of
M
and rotates its hyperbolic component around
i\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
. If we consider the action on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
through Möbius transformations we see that

{\rm Stab}_{{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})}(i)={\rm PSO}(2)

For
\theta\in[0,\pi)
the action (
3
) of a matrix
S=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\in{\rm PSO}(2)
can be interpreted as a clockwise rotation of angle
2\theta
around
i\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
. For every
S\in{\rm PSO}(2)
and every
\theta\in[0,\pi)
there is a unique way to write
S
as a rotation matrix of the form

S=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\sim\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\pi+\theta)&-\sin(\pi+\theta)\\
\sin(\pi+\theta)&\cos(\pi+\theta)\end{pmatrix}=-S

Given M positive definite with distinct eigenvalues we interpret the unique
S\in{\rm PSO}(2)
for which

SMS^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix},\ \lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
as the angle formed by the semi-axis
\{(0,y)|\ y>1\}
inside
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and the geodesic segment connecting the hyperbolic components of
{\rm Id}
and
M
(Figure
5
).

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 5.
Geometric interpretation of diagonalization matrix
S

In this paper we will use both the matrix and the angle notation: angle parameters will be denoted by
S
or
\alpha
depending on the context, where

S=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\frac{\alpha}{2}&-\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}\\
\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}&\cos\frac{\alpha}{2}\end{pmatrix},\alpha\in[0,2\pi)

Remark 2.25
.

(
Drawing angles "on the left"
) The matrix
S=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}
acts on
M
by clockwise rotation of center
{\rm Id}
and angle
2\theta
on the
\mathbb{H}^{2}
-component of the standard tube
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
. For this reason to draw the angle parameters we will consider the oriented geodesic going from
M
to
{\rm Id}
and draw the angle on the left of it.

Remark 2.26
.

For
\Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}
with
\lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2}
, the stabilizer of the quadruple
(0,{\rm Id},\Lambda,\infty)
is given by

\text{Stab}_{{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})}(0,{\rm Id},\Lambda,\infty)=\Big\{{\rm Id},\begin{pmatrix}r&0\\
0&r\end{pmatrix}\Big\}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}

where
r=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
. In the identification
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
the action of the matrix
\begin{pmatrix}r&0\\
0&r\end{pmatrix}
on
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
is a reflection across the
y
-axis of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Let
M=\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}&m_{2}\\
m_{2}&m_{3}\end{pmatrix}
be positive definite and identify
M
with
a+ib
in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
as in Section
2.7
. Then

rMr=\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}&-m_{2}\\
-m_{2}&m_{3}\end{pmatrix}\overset{(\lx@sectionsign\ref{sym})}{=}-a+ib\in\mathbb{H}^{2}

Put
M^{r}=rMr
. If
M
is a point of angle
\alpha\in[0,2\pi)
from
\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix},\ \lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
, then
M^{\emph{r}}
is a point of angle
(2\pi-\alpha)
from
\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix},\ \lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
(Figure
6
).

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 6.
The point
M^{r}
is obtained by reflecting
M
across the
y
-axis

To see this using the angle interpretation of diagonalization matrices take the unique
S_{1}\in{\rm PSO}(2)
diagonalizing
M
as in Lemma
2.24
. It holds:

(4)

S_{1}MS_{1}^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\ S_{1}rM^{r}rS_{1}^{T}

so that

\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}=r\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}r\overset{(\ref{clarity})}{=}\underbrace{rS_{1}r}_{S_{2}}M^{r}\underbrace{rS_{1}^{T}r}_{S_{2}^{T}}

If

S_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}

then

S_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(2\pi-\theta)&-\sin(2\pi-\theta)\\
\sin(2\pi-\theta)&\cos(2\pi-\theta)\end{pmatrix}

More generally if
A,B
are symmetric positive definite matrices such that
A^{-1}B
has distinct eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
, then the stabilizer
\text{Stab}_{{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})}(0,A,B,\infty)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
where the non-trivial element represents a reflection in the hyperbolic component of
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
across the geodesic going through
\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iA)
and
\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iB)
.

2.10.
Orientation of the hyperbolic component of
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}

Proposition 2.27
.

Consider the symmetric space
\mathcal{X}
associated to
{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
. Choosing an orientation of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
inside
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
is equivalent to choosing an orientation of
\mathbb{P}(\infty)\simeq\mathbb{P}(0)
where
\infty,0\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
.

Proof.

Fix a basis
\mathcal{B}=\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
and consider the two transverse Lagrangians

0=\langle e_{3},e_{4}\rangle
and
\infty=\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle
. The standard tube

\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}=\{iY|\ Y\in{\rm Sym}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})\}
is isometrically identified with
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
(Lemma
2.21
). The hyperbolic plane inside
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
is identified with the symmetric space associated to
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, that is
\mathcal{X}_{{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}=\{X\in{\rm Sym}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})|\ \det(X)=1\}
. All copies of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
inside
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
are canonically identified and stabilized by the set of matrices

(5)

\Big\{\begin{pmatrix}R&0\\
0&R\end{pmatrix},R\in{\rm O}(2)\Big\}

acting
{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
-equivariantly in the identification
\mathcal{X}\overset{\iota}{\mapsto}\mathbb{X}
. Let
\gamma(t)=\begin{pmatrix}ie^{t}&0\\
0&ie^{-t}\end{pmatrix}
be a geodesic ray
lying inside the hyperbolic component of
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
. Then
\gamma(t)
converges to the Lagrangian
l_{+}=\langle e_{1},e_{4}\rangle
when
t\to\infty
, and to the Lagrangian
l_{-}=\langle e_{2},e_{3}\rangle
when
t\to-\infty
. To fix an orientation of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
inside
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
it is sufficient to orient its visual boundary
\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{2}
. In Section
2.9
we have investigated the action of orthogonal matrices on
\mathbb{H}^{2}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
and we have interpreted matrices as angles in the hyperbolic component. The set in (
5
) acts preserving the Lagrangians
0
and
\infty
respectively and the visual boundary of the hyperbolic component is realized by the
{\rm O}(2)
-orbit of the Lagrangian
l_{+}
so that
\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{2}
is given by
\{g\cdot l_{+}\}
where
g
is as in (
5
). Any such point
g\cdot l_{+}
is a Lagrangian which intersects
\infty
in one line. We get the following identification:

\displaystyle\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{P}(\infty)

\displaystyle l

\displaystyle\mapsto l\cap\infty

To fix an orientation of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
inside
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
it is therefore sufficient to orient
\mathbb{P}(\infty)
.
This set is canonically identified with
\mathbb{P}(0)
. To see this let
v^{\perp\omega}=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{4}|\ \omega(v,u)=0\}
.

In particular
v\in v^{\perp\omega}
and dim(
v^{\perp\omega}
)=3. Then
\mathbb{P}(0)
and
\mathbb{P}(\infty)
are identified through the map
[v]\mapsto[v^{\perp\omega}\cap\infty]
.
∎

2.11.
Isometries reflecting the hyperbolic component

Proposition 2.28
.

For
A\in{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
let
f_{A}
be an isometry stabilizing the standard tube
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
:

\displaystyle f_{A}:\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}

\displaystyle iY

\displaystyle\mapsto iAYA^{T}

Then
f_{A}
is reversing the orientation of the hyperbolic component of
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
if and only if
\det A<0
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}=\chi_{{\rm GL}_{2,\mathbb{R}}}={\rm Sym}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}\times\chi_{{\rm SL}_{2,\mathbb{R}}}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
. The isometry
f_{A}
is linear in
Y
and its differential is the map
X\mapsto AXA^{T}
for any tangent vector
X=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}\\
x_{2}&x_{3}\end{pmatrix}
,
X\cong\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\\
x_{3}\end{pmatrix}
.
For
A=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&a_{2}\\
a_{3}&a_{4}\end{pmatrix}
the tangent vector
AXA^{T}=df_{A}(X)
can be rewritten as

\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}^{2}x_{1}+2a_{1}a_{2}x_{2}+a_{2}^{2}x_{3}\\
a_{1}a_{3}x_{1}+(a_{1}a_{4}+a_{2}a_{3})x_{2}+a_{2}a_{4}x_{3}\\
a_{3}^{2}x_{1}+2a_{3}a_{4}x_{2}+a_{4}^{2}x_{3}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}^{2}&2a_{1}a_{2}&a_{2}^{2}\\
a_{1}a_{3}&a_{1}a_{4}+a_{2}a_{3}&a_{2}a_{4}\\
a_{3}^{2}&2a_{3}a_{4}&a_{4}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\\
x_{3}\end{pmatrix}

where

\det\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}^{2}&2a_{1}a_{2}&a_{2}^{2}\\
a_{1}a_{3}&a_{1}a_{4}+a_{2}a_{3}&a_{2}a_{4}\\
a_{3}^{2}&2a_{3}a_{4}&a_{4}^{2}\end{pmatrix}=(\det A)^{3}

The map
f_{A}
is therefore reversing the orientation of the tube
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
if and only if
\det A<0
. To finish the proof we need to show that only the orientation of the hyperbolic component can be reversed, not the orientation of the
\mathbb{R}
-component. This holds as the action of a
f_{A}
on the
\mathbb{R}
-component of
iY\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
is a translation: if
\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(iY)
is the
\mathbb{R}
-component of
iY
then the
\mathbb{R}
-component of
iAYA^{T}
is given by
\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(iY)+\frac{2\log|\det A|}{\sqrt{2}}
.
∎

Remark 2.29
.

More generally given
l_{1},l_{2}
in
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{4})^{(2)}
and
g
an isometry stabilizing the tube
\mathcal{Y}_{l_{1},l_{2}}
, whether or not
g
is reversing the orientation of the hyperbolic component of
\mathcal{Y}_{l_{1},l_{2}}
is intrinsic and only depends on the sign of
\det A
.

Definition 2.30
.

An isometry
g\in{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
conjugate to
\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&A^{-T}\end{pmatrix}
with
A\in{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is called
reflecting
(resp.
non-reflecting
) if
\det A<0
(resp.
>0
).

3.
Parameters for quintuples

3.1.
The sets
\mathcal{Q}^{gen}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{st}

In this section we introduce parameters for generic quintuples.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(P,X,Y,Q)
be a maximal quadruple and let
\mu_{1},...,\mu_{n}
be the eigenvalues of the cross-ratio
R(P,X,Y,Q)
. The quadruple
(P,X,Y,Q)
is said to be
generic
if for any
i\neq j
it holds
\mu_{i}\neq\mu_{j}
.

Remark 3.2
.

Recall that we denote by
p_{P,Q}
the orthogonal projection on the tube
\mathcal{Y}_{P,Q}
. Let
\underline{b}=d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}(p_{P,Q}(X),p_{P,Q}(Y))
be the vector obtained by the orthogonal projection of
X
and
Y
on the tube
\mathcal{Y}_{P,Q}
. From Lemma
2.14
it is easy to see that the quadruple
(P,X,Y,Q)
is generic if and only if the vector
\underline{b}
is a regular vector of the Weyl chamber.

Definition 3.3
.

The set of
generic quintuples

\mathcal{Q}^{gen}
is given by:

\mathcal{Q}^{gen}:=\{(P,X,Y,Z,Q)\text{ maximal}|\ (P,X,Y,Q)\text{ and }(P,Y,Z,Q)\text{ generic }\}

Remark 3.4
.

Observe that the definition of generic quintuple strongly depends on the order of the quintuple: given
(P,X,Y,Z,Q)
generic it is not necessarily true that a cyclic permutation of the quintuple is generic.

We will see in the next section how the parametrization of
\mathcal{Q}^{gen}
is connected with the parametrization of right-angled hexagons of
\mathcal{X}
. Let us now consider the symmetric space associated to
{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
.

Definition 3.5
.

The set of
standard quintuples

\mathcal{Q}^{st}\subset\mathcal{Q}^{gen}
is given by

\mathcal{Q}^{st}:=\{(0,X,{\rm Id},\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix},\infty)\in\mathcal{Q}^{gen}|\ \lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R},\ \lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}\ \}

Remark 3.6
.

Recall that for a diagonal matrix
\Lambda
with different eigenvalues the stabilizer of
(0,{\rm Id},\Lambda,\infty)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
(see Remark
2.26
). For any
(P,X,Y,Z,Q)\in\mathcal{Q}^{gen}
we can always find a
g\in{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
such that
g(P,Y,Z,Q)=(0,{\rm Id},\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix},\infty)
where
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
. It is therefore clear that
\mathcal{Q}^{gen}/_{{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})}\cong\mathcal{Q}^{st}/_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}
.

We denote by
\mathfrak{a}
the set of regular vectors inside
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}

\mathfrak{a}=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|\ x_{1}>x_{2}>0\}

Proposition 3.7
.

The set
\mathcal{Q}^{gen}/_{{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})}
is parametrized by
\mathfrak{a}^{2}\times{\rm PSO}(2)/_{\sim}
where for
S
in
{\rm PSO}(2)
and
r=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
the equivalent relation is
S\sim rSr
.

The parametrization is given by

\Big((c_{1},c_{2}),(d_{1},d_{2}),[S]\Big)\mapsto\Big[\Big(0,S^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}\end{pmatrix}S,\ {\rm Id},\ \begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix},\infty\Big)\Big]\in\mathcal{Q}^{st}/_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}

with inverse

[(P,X,Y,Z,Q)]\mapsto\Big(\ (c_{1},c_{2})=d^{\mathfrak{a}^{+}}\big(p_{P,Q}(X),p_{P,Q}(Y)\big),(d_{1},d_{2})=d^{\mathfrak{a}^{+}}\big(p_{P,Q}(Y),p_{P,Q}(Z)\big),[S]\ \Big)

where

S(gX)S^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}\end{pmatrix},\ \frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}>\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}

and
g
is a map in
{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
such that
g(P,X,Y,Z,Q)\in\mathcal{Q}^{st}
.

The parameter space can be rewritten as
\mathfrak{a}^{2}\times[0,2\pi)/_{\sim}
where for
\alpha\in[0,2\pi)
the corresponding
{\rm PSO}(2)
-parameter is
S=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\frac{\alpha}{2}&-\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}\\
\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}&\cos\frac{\alpha}{2}\end{pmatrix}
and the equivalence relation is
\alpha\sim(2\pi-\alpha)
.

Proof.

We first show how to find parameters
(\underline{c},\underline{d},[S])
for a given quintuple
[(P,X,Y,Z,Q)]
in
\mathcal{Q}^{gen}/_{{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})}
. We want to use the fact:

\mathcal{Q}^{gen}/_{{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})}\cong\mathcal{Q}^{st}/_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}

Let
(P,X,Y,Z,Q)\in\mathcal{Q}^{gen}
. Up to isometry we can consider
P=0
and
Q=\infty
. Put

\underline{c}=(c_{1},c_{2})=d^{\mathfrak{a}^{+}}\big(p_{0,\infty}(X),p_{0,\infty}(Y)\big)\in\mathfrak{a}

\underline{d}=(d_{1},d_{2})=d^{\mathfrak{a}^{+}}\big(p_{0,\infty}(Y),p_{0,\infty}(Z)\big)\in\mathfrak{a}

Recall that the isometries stabilizing the standard tube are of the form
\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&A^{-T}\end{pmatrix}
where
A\in{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Let
g\in{\rm Stab}(\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
be such that

gY={\rm Id}
and
gZ=\begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}
(Figure
7
).

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 7.
The isometry
g
sends the quintuple
(P,X,Y,Z,Q)
to a standard one

The first equality forces
A=\mathcal{O}\sqrt{Y^{-1}}
where
\mathcal{O}\in{\rm PO}(2)
. The second equality forces
\mathcal{O}=P,Q
where
P
and
Q
are the unique matrices in
{\rm PSO}(2)
and
{\rm PO}(2)\backslash{\rm PSO}(2)
respectively (see Lemma
2.24
) such that

P(\sqrt{Y^{-1}}Z\sqrt{Y^{-1}})P^{T}=Q(\sqrt{Y^{-1}}Z\sqrt{Y^{-1}})Q^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}

Accordingly, the two only possibilities for
g
are:

g_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}P\sqrt{Y^{-1}}&0\\
0&P\sqrt{Y}\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }g_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}Q\sqrt{Y^{-1}}&0\\
0&Q\sqrt{Y}\end{pmatrix}

In particular we know
Q=rP
where
r=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
, so that
g_{2}X=rg_{1}Xr
.

Let
S
be the unique matrix in
{\rm PSO}(2)
such that
Sg_{1}XS^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}
for some
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
. Then
d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}(g_{1}X,{\rm Id})=(c_{1},c_{2})
so that
\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}
and
\lambda_{2}=\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}
.

On the other hand
S^{\prime}=rSr
is the unique matrix in
{\rm PSO}(2)
such that
S^{\prime}g_{2}XS^{\prime T}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}\end{pmatrix}
. The point
g_{2}X=rg_{1}Xr
is the image of
g_{1}X
under a reflection on the hyperbolic component (see Remark
2.26
) and the two quintuples

(0,g_{1}X,{\rm Id},\begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix},\infty)\ \ \text{ and }\ \ (0,g_{2}X,{\rm Id},\begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix},\infty)

are equivalent in
\mathcal{Q}^{st}/_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}
. The third parameter
[S]\in{\rm PSO}(2)/_{\sim}
is given by the diagonalization matrix and has the geometric interpretation of an angle: given
S\in{\rm PSO}(2)
we write it as a matrix
S=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\frac{\alpha}{2}&-\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}\\
\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}&\cos\frac{\alpha}{2}\end{pmatrix},\alpha\in[0,2\pi)
and the equivalence relation is the identification of angle
\alpha
with angle
(2\pi-\alpha)
(see Figure
6
). We obtain parameters

(\underline{c},\underline{d},[\alpha])
in
\mathfrak{a}^{2}\times[0,2\pi)/_{\sim}
. The parameter
S
or
\alpha
will be called the
angle parameter
of the generic quintuple and provides information about the angle between the hyperbolic components of
X
and
Z
. We will draw the angle on the left as explained in Remark
2.25
.

For the inverse map, to any element of the parameter space
\mathfrak{a}^{2}\times{\rm PSO}(2)/_{\sim}
we can associate a unique quintuple inside
\mathcal{Q}^{st}/_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}
. To
\big((c_{1},c_{2}),(d_{1},d_{2}),[S]\big)
in
\mathfrak{a}^{2}\times{\rm PSO}(2)/_{\sim}
we associate the standard quintuple

(P,X,Y,Z,Q)=\Big(0,S^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}\end{pmatrix}S,\ {\rm Id},\ \begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix},\infty\Big)

Then
X
is a matrix such that
d^{\mathfrak{a}^{+}}(iX,i{\rm Id})=(c_{1},c_{2})
and
SXS^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}\end{pmatrix}
. For any
S^{\prime}\sim S
we obtain an equivalent quintuple
(P,X^{\prime},Y,Z,Q)
inside
\mathcal{Q}^{st}/_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}
where

(P,X^{\prime},Y,Z,Q)=(P,rXr,Y,Z,Q)=\begin{pmatrix}r&0\\
0&r\end{pmatrix}\cdot(P,X,Y,Z,Q)

∎

Corollary 3.8
.

The set
\mathcal{Q}^{gen}/_{{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})}
is parametrized by
\mathfrak{a}^{2}\times[0,\pi]
.

Proof.

The equivalence relation of Proposition
3.7
is given by
\alpha\sim 2\pi-\alpha
. We can always choose
\alpha\in[0,\pi]
as representative of the equivalence class.
∎

To conclude this section we state two technical lemmas that will be useful later.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
p=\big((c_{1},c_{2}),(d_{1},d_{2}),[S]\big)\in\mathfrak{a}^{2}\times{\rm PSO}(2)/_{\sim}
and let
X,Y
be positive definite such that
d^{\mathfrak{a}^{+}}(iX,iY)=(c_{1},c_{2})
. Then the unique
Z
such that
(0,X,Y,Z,\infty)
corresponds to
p
in the parametrization of Proposition
3.7
is given by

Z=\sqrt{Y}R^{T}S\begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}S^{T}R\sqrt{Y}

where
R
is the unique matrix in
{\rm PSO}(2)
such that
R(\sqrt{Y}^{-1}X\sqrt{Y}^{-1})R^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}\end{pmatrix}
.

Proof.

It is easy to check that for such a
Z
it holds

d^{\mathfrak{a}^{+}}(iY,iZ)=(d_{1},d_{2})
. By Proposition
3.7
we know that
SgXS^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}
where
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
and where
g
is such that
g(0,X,Y,Z,\infty)
is a standard quintuple i.e.

gY={\rm Id}
and
gZ=\begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}
. Then
g=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&A^{-T}\end{pmatrix}
where
A=S^{T}R\sqrt{Y}^{-1}
and
gX=S^{T}R\sqrt{Y}^{-1}X\sqrt{Y}^{-1}SR^{T}
. It holds

S(gX)S^{T}=R(\sqrt{Y}^{-1}X\sqrt{Y}^{-1})R^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}\end{pmatrix}

To finish the proof we need to check that for
S^{\prime}\sim S
we obtain the same point in
\mathcal{Q}^{gen}/_{{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})}
. Take
S^{\prime}=rSr
, where
r=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
. Then
Z^{\prime}=\sqrt{Y}R^{T}S^{\prime}\begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}S^{\prime T}R\sqrt{Y}
.
Consider

h=\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{Y}R^{T}S^{\prime}rS^{T}R\sqrt{Y}^{-1}&0\\
0&\sqrt{Y}^{-1}R^{T}S^{\prime}rS^{T}R\sqrt{Y}\end{pmatrix}

Then
h(0,X,Y,Z,\infty)=(0,X,Y,Z^{\prime},\infty)
so that
[(0,X,Y,Z,\infty)]=[(0,X,Y,Z^{\prime},\infty)]
in
\mathcal{Q}^{gen}/_{{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})}
. Geometrically the map
h
is a reflection in the
\mathbb{H}^{2}
-component across the geodesic passing through
\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iX)
and
\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iY)
, denoted for simplicity by
X
and
Y
respectively. This is shown in Figure
8
below.
∎

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 8.
The map
h
in the Poincaré disk model of
\mathbb{H}^{2}

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
p=\big((c_{1},c_{2}),(d_{1},d_{2}),[S]\big)
be inside
\mathfrak{a}^{2}\times{\rm PSO}(2)/_{\sim}
and let
Y,Z
be positive definite such that
d^{\mathfrak{a}^{+}}(iY,iZ)=(d_{1},d_{2})
. Then the unique
X
such that
(0,X,Y,Z,\infty)
corresponds to
p
in the parametrization of Proposition
3.7
is given by

X=\sqrt{Y}P^{T}S^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}\end{pmatrix}SP\sqrt{Y}

where
P
is the unique matrix in
{\rm PSO}(2)
such that
P(\sqrt{Y}^{-1}Z\sqrt{Y}^{-1})P^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}
.

Proof.

It is easy to check that for such
X
it holds

d^{\mathfrak{a}^{+}}(iX,iY)=(c_{1},c_{2})
. Take
g=\begin{pmatrix}P\sqrt{Y}^{-1}&0\\
0&P\sqrt{Y}\end{pmatrix}
then
gY={\rm Id}
,
gZ=\begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}
and
gX=P\sqrt{Y}^{-1}X\sqrt{Y}^{-1}P^{T}=S^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}\end{pmatrix}S
, so that

S(g\cdot X)S^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}\end{pmatrix}

To finish the proof we need to check that for
S^{\prime}\sim S
we obtain the same point in
\mathcal{Q}^{gen}/_{{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})}
. Take
S^{\prime}=rSr
where
r=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
. Then

X^{\prime}=\sqrt{Y}P^{T}S^{\prime T}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}\end{pmatrix}S^{\prime}P\sqrt{Y}=\sqrt{Y}P^{T}rS^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{c_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{c_{1}}}\end{pmatrix}SrP\sqrt{Y}

Consider

h=\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{Y}P^{T}rP\sqrt{Y}^{-1}&0\\
0&\sqrt{Y}^{-1}P^{T}rP\sqrt{Y}\end{pmatrix}

Then
h(0,X,Y,Z,\infty)=(0,X^{\prime},Y,Z,\infty)

so that
[(0,X,Y,Z,\infty)]=[(0,X^{\prime},Y,Z,\infty)]
in
\mathcal{Q}^{gen}/_{{\rm PSp}(4,R)}
. Geometrically the map
h
can be seen as a reflection in the
\mathbb{H}^{2}
-component across the geodesic passing through
Y
and
Z
(similar to Figure
8
).
∎

4.
Parameters for right-angled hexagons

In this section we define ordered right-angled hexagons in the Siegel space
\mathcal{X}
. We distinguish between generic and non-generic hexagons and introduce a parameter space for both. A parameter space which encloses both cases is given in Theorem
4.25
.

4.1.
Definition of hexagon, the sets
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}^{gen}
and
\mathcal{H}^{st}

Definition 4.1
.

A
right-angled hexagon
in
\mathcal{X}
is a cyclic sequence of six
\mathbb{R}
-tubes

H=[\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{4},\mathcal{Y}_{5},\mathcal{Y}_{6}]
where any two consecutive tubes are orthogonal and such that

\mathcal{Y}_{1}=\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}},\ \mathcal{Y}_{2}=\mathcal{Y}_{Q_{1},Q_{2}},\ \mathcal{Y}_{3}=\mathcal{Y}_{P_{3},P_{4}},\ \mathcal{Y}_{4}=\mathcal{Y}_{Q_{3},Q_{4}},\ \mathcal{Y}_{5}=\mathcal{Y}_{P_{5},P_{6}},\ \mathcal{Y}_{6}=\mathcal{Y}_{Q_{5},Q_{6}}

for a maximal 12-tuple
(P_{1},Q_{6},Q_{1},P_{2},P_{3},Q_{2},Q_{3},P_{4},P_{5},Q_{4},Q_{5},P_{6})
.

The maximal 12-tuple determining a right hexagon
H
in
\mathcal{X}
is illustrated in Figure
9
.

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 9.
The maximal 12-tuple determining the right-angled hexagon
H=[\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{4},\mathcal{Y}_{5},\mathcal{Y}_{6}]

Definition 4.2
.

Let
H=[\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{4},\mathcal{Y}_{5},\mathcal{Y}_{6}]
be a right-angled hexagon in
\mathcal{X}
. We define the
stabilizer of
H
and denote it by
{\rm Stab}(H)
the stabilizer

{\rm Stab}(H)=\big\{g\in{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})|\ g\cdot\mathcal{Y}_{i}=\mathcal{Y}_{i},\ i\in\{1,...,6\}\big\}

Definition 4.3
.

The set
\mathcal{H}
of
ordered right-angled hexagons
in
\mathcal{X}
is defined by

\mathcal{H}:=\{(H,\mathcal{Y}_{1})|\ H=[\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{4},\mathcal{Y}_{5},\mathcal{Y}_{6}]\text{ right-angled hexagon }\}

We want to be able to determine a point
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{1})
inside
\mathcal{H}
by giving the data of an ordered maximal 6-tuple. There are many ways to do this, as explained in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
H=[\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{4},\mathcal{Y}_{5},\mathcal{Y}_{6}]
be a right-angled hexagon with associated maximal 12-tuple
(P_{1},Q_{6},Q_{1},P_{2},P_{3},Q_{2},Q_{3},P_{4},P_{5},Q_{4},Q_{5},P_{6})
.
Then
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{1})\in\mathcal{H}
is uniquely determined by the following ordered maximal 6-tuples:

(6)

\displaystyle(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4},P_{5},P_{6})

(7)

\displaystyle(Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},Q_{4},Q_{5},Q_{6})

(8)

\displaystyle(P_{2},Q_{2},P_{4},P_{5},Q_{5},P_{1})

Proof.

Given the maximal 6-tuple
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4},P_{5},P_{6})
we use Lemma
2.17
to uniquely determine
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},Q_{4},Q_{5},Q_{6}
such that

(9)

\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Q_{1},Q_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{P_{3},P_{4}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Q_{3},Q_{4}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{P_{5},P_{6}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Q_{6},Q_{5}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}}

The hexagon
H
is determined by the
\mathbb{R}
-tubes in (
9
) and we put
\mathcal{Y}_{1}=\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}}
. The quadruples
(P_{1},Q_{6},Q_{1},P_{2}),(P_{3},Q_{2},Q_{3},P_{4}),(P_{5},Q_{4},Q_{5},P_{6})
are maximal by Lemma
2.18
and we obtain a maximal 12-tuple
(P_{1},Q_{6},Q_{1},P_{2},P_{3},Q_{2},Q_{3},P_{4},P_{5},Q_{4},Q_{5},P_{6})
by Lemma
2.3
. The proof for the 6-tuple in (
7
) is similar and we put again
\mathcal{Y}_{1}=\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}}
where the 6-tuple
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4},P_{5},P_{6})
is uniquely determined by the orthogonality conditions in (
9
). Given
(P_{2},Q_{2},P_{4},P_{5},Q_{5},P_{1})
maximal
we construct the hexagon
H
as following: let
g\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
such that
(gP_{1},gP_{2})=(\infty,0)
. Let us denote

gQ_{2}=A,\ gP_{4}=B,\ gP_{5}=C,\ gQ_{5}=D

We use Lemma
2.17
and Lemma
2.19
to uniquely determine the right-angled hexagon
H_{0,A,B,C,D,\infty}
as shown in in Figure
10
below. The maximality of the 12-tuple at the boundary is again guaranteed by Lemma
2.18
. We put
H=g^{-1}(H_{0,A,B,C,D,\infty})
and
\mathcal{Y}_{1}=\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}}
.
∎

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 10.
The right-angled hexagon
H_{0,A,B,C,D,\infty}
is uniquely determined by the maximal 6-tuple
(0,A,B,C,D,\infty)

Notation 4.5
.

In this paper we will use a maximal 6-tuple as in (
8
) to uniquely determine a right-angled hexagon
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{1})
inside
\mathcal{H}
. In order to simplify the notation we will write

H=(P,A,B,C,D,Q)

to refer to a hexagon
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{P,Q})
where
H
is uniquely determined by
(P,A,B,C,D,Q)
as in (
8
). The choice of the tube
\mathcal{Y}_{P,Q}
is encoded in the order of the maximal 6-tuple.
When
P=0
and
Q=\infty
then
A,B,C,D
are positive definite and we obtain a hexagon
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
where
H
is shown in Figure
10
. In particular the maximal 12-tuple associated to
H
is given by

H=(\infty,-D,-A,0,AB^{-1}A,A,Z_{1},B,C,Z_{2},D,DC^{-1}D)

where
Z_{1},Z_{2}
are uniquely defined by requiring
\mathcal{Y}_{AB^{-1}A,B}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Z_{1},Z_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{C,DC^{-1}D}
.

Definition 4.6
.

The set of
generic hexagons

\mathcal{H}^{gen}\subset\mathcal{H}
is given by ordered 6-tuples of the form

\mathcal{H}^{gen}:=\{(P,A,B,C,D,Q)\text{ maximal}|\ (P,A,B,Q),(P,B,C,Q),(P,C,D,Q)\text{ generic}\}

Remark 4.7
.

Let
H=(P,A,B,C,D,Q)
be a generic hexagon. Project
A,B,C
and
D
orthogonally on
\mathcal{Y}_{P,Q}
and denote by
\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d}
the vectors
d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}(p_{P,Q}(A),p_{P,Q}(B))
,
d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}(p_{P,Q}(B),p_{P,Q}(C))
and
d^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}}(p_{P,Q}(C),p_{P,Q}(D))
respectively (Figure
11
). It is easy to see that the hexagon
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{P,Q})
is generic if and only if the vectors
\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d}
are regular.

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 11.
The hexagon
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{1})
is generic if and only if
\underline{b},\underline{c}
and
\underline{d}
are regular

Let us now focus on the symmetric space associated to
{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
.

Definition 4.8
.

The set of
standard hexagons

\mathcal{H}^{st}\subset\mathcal{H}^{gen}
is given by

\mathcal{H}^{st}:=\{(0,A,{\rm Id},\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix},D,\infty)\in\mathcal{H}^{gen}|\ \lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R},\ \lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}\}

Remark 4.9
.

Similarly to what we have seen for quintuples for any
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{1})
in
\mathcal{H}^{gen}
we can always find an isometry
g\in{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
such that
(gH,g\mathcal{Y}_{1})\in\mathcal{H}^{st}
. For a diagonal matrix
\Lambda
with different eigenvalues the stabilizer of
(0,{\rm Id},\Lambda,\infty)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
(Remark
2.26
). It holds
\mathcal{H}^{gen}/_{{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})}\cong\mathcal{H}^{st}/_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}
.

4.2.
Non-generic hexagons: the set
\mathcal{H}^{nongen}

We define three different types of non-generic hexagons depending on how many quadruples in
H=(P,A,B,C,D,Q)
fail to be generic: a non-generic hexagon of type
k
is a hexagon where
k
quadruples are non-generic.

Definition 4.10
.

(
Non-generic quadruple
) Let
(P,X,Y,Q)
be a maximal quadruple and let
(\mu_{1},\,\mu_{2})
be the eigenvalues of the cross-ratio
R(P,X,Y,Q)
. The quadruple
(P,X,Y,Q)
is said to be
non-generic
if
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
.

Definition 4.11
.

The set
\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type1}
is given by

\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type1}:=\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type1.1}\cup\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type1.2}\cup\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type1.3}

where

\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type1.1}:=\{(P,A,B,C,D,Q)\text{ maximal}|\ (P,A,B,Q)\text{ non-generic},(P,B,C,Q),(P,C,D,Q)\text{ generic}\}

\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type1.2}:=\{(P,A,B,C,D,Q)\text{ maximal}|\ (P,B,C,Q)\text{ non-generic},(P,A,B,Q),(P,C,D,Q)\text{ generic}\}

\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type1.3}:=\{(P,A,B,C,D,Q)\text{ maximal}|\ (P,C,D,Q)\text{ non-generic},(P,A,B,Q),(P,B,C,Q)\text{ generic}\}

Definition 4.12
.

The set
\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type2}
is given by

\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type2}:=\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type2.1}\cup\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type2.2}\cup\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type2.3}

where

\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type2.1}:=\{(P,A,B,C,D,Q)\text{ maximal}|\ (P,A,B,Q),(P,B,C,Q)\text{ non-generic},(P,C,D,Q)\text{ generic}\}

\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type2.2}:=\{(P,A,B,C,D,Q)\text{ maximal}|\ (P,A,B,Q),(P,C,D,Q)\text{ non-generic},(P,B,C,Q)\text{ generic}\}

\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type2.3}:=\{(P,A,B,C,D,Q)\text{ maximal}|\ (P,B,C,Q),(P,C,D,Q)\text{ non-generic},(P,A,B,Q)\text{ generic}\}

Definition 4.13
.

The set
\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type3}
is given by

\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type3}:=\{(P,A,B,C,D,Q)\text{ maximal}|\ (P,A,B,Q),(P,B,C,Q),(P,C,D,Q)\text{ non-generic}\}

Proposition 4.14
.

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{gen}\cup\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type1}\cup\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type2}\cup\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type3}

Proof.

The inclusion
\mathcal{H}^{gen}\cup\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type1}\cup\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type2}\cup\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type3}\subset\mathcal{H}
is trivial.

Let
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{1})\in\mathcal{H}
. By Lemma
4.4
we can uniquely determine
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{1})
from a maximal 6-tuple
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{1})=(P,A,B,C,D,Q)
where
\mathcal{Y}_{1}=\mathcal{Y}_{P,Q}
. Let
\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d}
be the three vectors in Figure
11
. The hexagon
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{1})
is generic if and only if the vectors
\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d}
are regular and is non-generic if one of them is inside
\mathfrak{d}
. For each vector
\underline{b},\underline{c}
and
\underline{d}
we see if it is generic or not and we list all possible configurations. The hexagon
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{1})
must be contained in one of this exhaustive list.
We obtain
2^{3}=8
possible configurations, one in
\mathcal{H}^{gen}
, three in
\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type1}
, three in
\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type2}
and one in
\mathcal{H}^{nongen}_{type3}
.
∎

4.3.
Arc coordinates for generic hexagons

In this section we parametrize generic hexagons in
\mathcal{X}
up to isometry. We will concentrate on the case where
\mathcal{X}
is the symmetric space associated to
{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
. Recall that
\mathfrak{a}
denotes the set of regular vectors inside
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}
.

Proposition 4.15
.

The set
\mathcal{H}^{gen}/_{{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})}
is parametrized by
\mathfrak{a}^{3}\times{\rm PSO}(2)^{2}/_{\sim}
where for
(S_{1},S_{2})\in{\rm PSO}(2)^{2}
and
r=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
the equivalent relation is
(S_{1},S_{2})\sim(rS_{1}r,rS_{2}r)
.

The parametrization is given by

(\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d},[S_{1},S_{2}])\mapsto[(0,A,{\rm Id},C,D,\infty)]\in\mathcal{H}^{st}/_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}

where
\underline{b}=(b_{1},b_{2}),\ \underline{c}=(c_{1},c_{2}),\ \underline{d}=(d_{1},d_{2})
and

A=S_{1}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{e^{b_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{e^{b_{1}}}\end{pmatrix}S_{1}\\

C=\begin{pmatrix}e^{c_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{c_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\\

D=\begin{pmatrix}0&\sqrt{e^{c_{1}}}\\
-\sqrt{e^{c_{2}}}&0\end{pmatrix}S_{2}\begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}S_{2}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&-\sqrt{e^{c_{2}}}\\
\sqrt{e^{c_{1}}}&0\end{pmatrix}

The parameter space can be rewritten as
\mathfrak{a}^{3}\times[0,2\pi)^{2}/_{\sim}
where for
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in[0,2\pi)^{2}
the corresponding
{\rm PSO}(2)^{2}
-parameter is
(S_{1},S_{2})
with
S_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\frac{\alpha_{i}}{2}&-\sin\frac{\alpha_{i}}{2}\\
