). This is explained in Section
5.4
.
∎

6.4.
Geometrical interpretation of the set
\mathcal{K}

In this section we give a geometrical interpretation to the reflection set associated to the side of a hexagon (and in particular to the set
\mathcal{K}
) in terms of the polygonal chain associated to the hexagon. For simplicity we will consider an ordered hexagon of the form
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
and will study the associated polygonal chain defined in
4.19
.

Definition 6.21
.

Two right-angled hexagons
H_{1},H_{2}
are said to be
adjacent at
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
if

H_{1}=[\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{4},\mathcal{Y}_{5},\mathcal{Y}_{6}]
and
H_{2}=[\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{6},\mathcal{Y}_{7},\mathcal{Y}_{8},\mathcal{Y}_{9},\mathcal{Y}_{2}]
. Two such adjacent hexagons will be denoted
H_{1}\#_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}H_{2}
.

When two hexagons are adjacent they share one vertex of the correspondent polygonal chains and we can look at the "attached" polygonal chains.

Definition 6.22
.

Let
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
and
(H^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
be two adjacent right-angled hexagons where

(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})=(0,A,B,C,D,\infty)

(H^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})=(0,A^{\prime},B^{\prime},C^{\prime},D^{\prime},\infty),\ D^{\prime}=A

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 29.
The two adjacent hexagons
H
and
H^{\prime}

We can look at the ordered sequence of points

\Big(\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iA^{\prime}),\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iB^{\prime}),\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iC^{\prime}),\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iA),\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iB),\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iC),\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iD)\Big)

obtained by the union of vertices of the polygonal chains associated to
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
and
(H^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
respectively. This induces an orientation on the segments forming the polygonal chains. The
attachment angle

\beta
between these two polygonal chains is the angle (measured on the left) formed by the two (non-vanishing) segments attached at the point
\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iD^{\prime})=\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iA)
.

For a visualization of the attachment angle see for example Figure
31
. We want to study the case where
H^{\prime}
is obtained by reflecting
H
along a side.

Definition 6.23
.

Let
H_{1}=[\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{4},\mathcal{Y}_{5},\mathcal{Y}_{6}]
and
H_{2}=[\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{6},\mathcal{Y}_{7},\mathcal{Y}_{8},\mathcal{Y}_{9},\mathcal{Y}_{2}]
be two hexagons adjacent at
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
. The hexagons
H_{1}\#_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}H_{2}
are said to be
symmetric
if
H_{2}=R(H_{1})
for a reflection
R\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{6},\mathcal{Y}_{2}}
.

Our aim is to give a geometric interpretation of the set
\mathcal{K}
. More precisely we show how the choice of
R\in\mathcal{K}
is equivalent to choosing an attachment angle
\beta
between the polygonal chains of
H
and
\overline{R}(H)
, where
H\#\overline{R}(H)
are adjacent symmetric and
\overline{R}
is conjugate to
R
. We start by stating a proposition which will be useful later.

Proposition 6.24
.

Let
(0,P,Q,\infty)
be a maximal quadruple and consider the orthogonal tubes
\mathcal{Y}_{-Q,Q}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{P,QP^{-1}Q}
. Suppose
(-Q,0,P,Q)
generic. Then the hyperbolic components of
P,Q
and
QP^{-1}Q
lie on the same geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and
\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(p_{0,\infty}(Q))
is the middle point of the three. If
(-Q,0,P,Q)
is non-generic then the hyperbolic components coincide in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.6
we know that
(-Q,0,P,Q)
is generic if and only if the quadruple
(0,Q,QP^{-1}Q,\infty)
is generic. Up to
{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
-action we can consider
Q={\rm Id}
and
QP^{-1}Q=Y
where
Y=\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}&0\\
0&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}
,
y_{1}>y_{2}
. The hyperbolic component of
{\rm Id}
is
i\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
in the identification of Section
2.8
. It is trivial to show that the hyperbolic components of
Y
and
Y^{-1}
lie on the same geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
(the
y
-axis), where the point
i
is in the middle. Since isometries preserve geodesics, the same is true more generally for tubes
\mathcal{Y}_{-Q,Q}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{P,PQP^{-1}Q}
. The non-generic case is trivial.
∎

We also recall a standard fact of linear algebra.

Lemma 6.25
.

Let
K\in{\rm PO}(2)
such that
K^{2}={\rm Id}
. Then

K={\rm Id}\ \ \text{ or }\ \ K=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}

for a unique
\theta\in[0,\pi)
.

Let us denote by
\beta
the following map

(16)

\displaystyle\beta:\mathcal{K}

\displaystyle\to[0,2\pi)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-K&0\\
0&K\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}\pi+2\theta\\
\pi\ \ \text{ if }K={\rm Id}\end{cases}

where

K=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix},\ \theta\in[0,\pi)

Proposition 6.26
.

Let
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})\in\mathcal{H}
where
H=(0,A,{\rm Id},C,D,\infty)
and has arc coordinates
(\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d},[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}])
. Suppose the angles of the polygonal chain associated to
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
are
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
(possibly only
\alpha
or no angle).
Consider a reflection
\overline{R}
inside
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}_{-A,A}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty},\mathcal{Y}_{A^{2},{\rm Id}}}
:

\overline{R}=g^{-1}Rg\ \in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}_{-A,A}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty},\mathcal{Y}_{A^{2},{\rm Id}}}

for
R=\begin{pmatrix}-K&0\\
0&K\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{K}
and
g
an isometry such that
g(-A,0,A^{2},A)=(0,{\rm Id},Y,\infty)
with
Y
diagonal.

Then the attachment angle between the polygonal chains of
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
and
(\overline{R}(H),\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
is given by
\beta(K)
where
\beta
is the map in (
16
). Moreover the polygonal chain associated to
(\overline{R}(H),\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
has

(i)

segments of lengths
h(\underline{d}),h(\underline{c}),h(\underline{b})
where
h
is the map
h(d_{1},d_{2})=d_{1}-d_{2}

(ii)

angles (if there):

\begin{cases}\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}\ \ (or\ \alpha)\\
2\pi-\alpha_{2},2\pi-\alpha_{1}(or\ 2\pi-\alpha)\ \text{ if }K={\rm Id}\end{cases}

Proof.

Let us first consider the case where
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
is generic. The two adjacent symmetric hexagons are illustrated in Figure
30
. By Proposition
6.17
the 6-tuple

(0,\overline{R}(D),\overline{R}(C),\overline{R}({\rm Id})=A^{2},\overline{R}(A)=A,\infty)
is maximal. This 6-tuple determines the ordered sequence of vertices in the polygonal chain associated to
(\overline{R}(H),\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
.

By Corollary

we know

\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}_{-A,A}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty},\mathcal{Y}_{A^{2},{\rm Id}}}=\begin{cases}\{g^{-1}R_{st}g,g^{-1}R_{ex}g\},\ if\ \underline{b}\in\mathfrak{a}\\
g^{-1}\mathcal{K}g\ if\ \underline{b}\in\mathfrak{d}\end{cases}

where

. As

as

\overline{R}_{st}(-A)=-A,\ \ \overline{R}_{st}(A)=A,\ \ \overline{R}_{st}(0)=\infty,\ \ \overline{R}_{st}(A^{2})={\rm Id}

It is straightforward to see that the segments of the polygonal chain associated to

E_{1}^{\overline{R}_{st}}=A,\ \ E_{-1}^{\overline{R}_{st}}=-A

By Proposition
6.13

\overline{R}_{st}

X\pitchfork A

\overline{R}_{st}(X)

. By Proposition

the hyperbolic components of

and

of an angle

around

. This is illustrated on the left side of Figure

where the polygonal chain of

f\circ\overline{R}_{st}(-A)=-A,\ \ f\circ\overline{R}_{st}(A)=A,\ \ f\circ\overline{R}_{st}=\infty,\ \ f\circ\overline{R}_{st}(A^{2})={\rm Id}

so that
\overline{R}_{ex}=f\circ\overline{R}_{st}

Remark 6.27
.

If
H
is contained in a maximal polydisk it can happen that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}_{-A,A}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty},\mathcal{Y}_{A^{2},{\rm Id}}}
contains two different reflections
\overline{R},\overline{R^{\prime}}
for which
\overline{R}(H)=\overline{R^{\prime}}(H)
. Let us denote for simplicity
p=(\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d},[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}])
the arc coordinates associated to
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
. By the geometrical interpretation of Proposition
6.26
it is not hard to show that the case
\overline{R}(H)=\overline{R^{\prime}}(H)
happens exactly for

(17)

\overline{R},\overline{R^{\prime}}\in\{g^{-1}R_{st}g,\ g^{-1}R_{ex}g\}\ \ \text{ if }p\in\mathcal{D}\backslash\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}

and for any

(18)

\overline{R},\overline{R^{\prime}}\in g^{-1}\mathcal{K}g\ \ \text{ if }p\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}

The two hexagons
H
and
\overline{R}(H)=\overline{R^{\prime}}(H)
lie both inside the model polydisk if
\overline{R},\overline{R^{\prime}}
are as in (
17
) and all the points of the two polygonal chains lie on the vertical geodesic of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. In (
18
), the two hexagons
H
and
\overline{R}(H)=\overline{R^{\prime}}(H)
lie both inside the diagonal disc and all the points of the polygonal chains coincide with
\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(i{\rm Id})
.

Remark 6.28
.

In Proposition
6.26
we have shown how to draw the polygonal associated to
(\overline{R}_{2}(H),\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
where
\overline{R}_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}_{-A,A}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty},\mathcal{Y}_{A^{2},{\rm Id}}}
. We can state a similar result for
\overline{R}_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}_{-D,D}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty},\mathcal{Y}_{C,DC^{-1}D}}
.
We can then draw the three attached polygonal chains
\overline{R}_{2}(H)\#H\#\overline{R}_{1}(H)
. Figure
33
illustrates all possible polygonal chains of
\overline{R}_{2}(H)\#H\#\overline{R}_{1}(H)
in the case that
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
is a generic right-angled hexagon. The polygonal chains are drawn up to isometry, this means that we consider two polygonal chains to be equivalent if there exists an isometry
g\in{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
sending all the vertices of one to the vertices of the other.

7.
Parameters for maximal representations

In this section we use arc coordinates of right-angled hexagons to parametrize maximal representations. We start by discussing geometric properties of Shilov hyperbolic isometries in
{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
. We introduce the notion of a maximal representation from the reflection group

\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
into
{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(2n,\mathbb{R})
. This will lead to the parametrization of a subset of maximal representations arising from a very geometric picture (Theorem
7.18
).

7.1.
Shilov hyperbolic isometries

Definition 7.1
.

An element
g\in{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
is called
Shilov hyperbolic
if it is conjugate to
\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&A^{-T}\end{pmatrix}
for a matrix
A\in{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
with complex eigenvalues with modulus greater than one.

An element
g\in{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
is Shilov hyperbolic if and only if
g
fixes two transverse Lagrangians
l_{g}^{+},l_{g}^{-}
on which it acts expandingly and contractingly respectively. In the following example we classify Shilov hyperbolic elements in
{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
.

Example 7.2
.

Let
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
be the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Recall that we denote
0,l_{\infty}
the Lagrangians
0=\langle e_{3},e_{4}\rangle
and
l_{\infty}=\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle
respectively, and that the standard tube
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}=\{iY|\ Y\in{\rm Sym}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})\}

is isometrically identified with
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
(see Lemma
2.21
) through the map

\displaystyle\pi^{\mathbb{R}}\times\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}:\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}\times{\rm Sym}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle iY

\displaystyle\mapsto\Big(\frac{\log\det Y}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{Y}{\sqrt{\det Y}}\Big)

Moreover, in the proof of Proposition
2.27
we have shown how the visual boundary of the hyperbolic component of
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
can be realized as the
{\rm O}(2)
-orbit of the Lagrangian
l=\langle e_{1},e_{4}\rangle
.
An isometry
g_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&A^{-T}\end{pmatrix}
in
{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
stabilizes the standard tube
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
. The action of
g_{A}
on the
\mathbb{R}
-component of
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
is given by

\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(g_{A}(iY))=\frac{\log\det^{2}(A)\det Y}{\sqrt{2}}=\frac{\log\det^{2}(A)}{\sqrt{2}}+\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(iY)

We study the action of
g_{A}
on the hyperbolic component of the standard tube
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
and give a geometric interpretation for
g_{A}
to be Shilov hyperbolic. We have the following possibilities:

•

A
has one eigenvalues
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and is conjugate to a matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\lambda\end{pmatrix}&0\\
0&\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\lambda}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\lambda}\end{pmatrix}\end{pmatrix}\sim g_{A}

The isometry
g_{A}
acts on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
as the identity map and
\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(g_{A}(iY))-\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(iY)=\frac{4\log|\lambda|}{\sqrt{2}}
. It is clear that
g_{A}
is Shilov hyperbolic if and only if
|\lambda|\neq 1
that is
g_{A}\neq id
.

•

A
has eigenvalues
\lambda>\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and is conjugate to a matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\mu\end{pmatrix}&0\\
0&\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\lambda}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\mu}\end{pmatrix}\end{pmatrix}\sim g_{A}

The isometry
g_{A}
acts on the hyperbolic component of
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
as an hyperbolic isometry, it fixes exactly two points in the boundary of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. The axis is the infinite geodesic on the
\mathbb{H}^{2}
-component having as endpoints the two fixed points and has translation length given by
\log|\frac{\lambda}{\mu}|
. Observe that if
\lambda
and
\mu
have different signs then
\det A<0
and
g_{A}
is reversing the orientation of the
\mathbb{H}^{2}
-component (see Definition
2.30
). On the
\mathbb{R}
-component we have
\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(g_{A}(iY))-\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(iY)=\frac{2\log|\lambda\mu|}{\sqrt{2}}
. We should think at the isometry
g_{A}
being Shilov hyperbolic if we can find a point in the tube that moves vertically (on the
\mathbb{R}
-component) more than horizontally (on the
\mathbb{H}^{2}
-component). This will be explained in detail in Lemma
7.3
.

•

A
has one eigenvalues
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and is conjugate to a matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&1\\
0&\lambda\end{pmatrix}&0\\
0&\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\lambda}&0\\
-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}&\frac{1}{\lambda}\end{pmatrix}\end{pmatrix}\sim g_{A}

The map
g_{A}
acts on the hyperbolic component of
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
as a parabolic isometry, it fixes exactly one point in the boundary of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. We have
\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(g_{A}(iY))-\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(iY)=\frac{4\log|\lambda|}{\sqrt{2}}
. The isometry
g_{A}
is Shilov hyperbolic if and only if
|\lambda|\neq 1
.

•

A
has two complex eigenvalues
\lambda e^{i\theta},\lambda e^{-i\theta},\theta\neq 2k\pi
and is conjugate to a matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda\cos\theta&-\lambda\sin\theta\\
\lambda\sin\theta&\lambda\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}&0\\
0&\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\lambda}\cos\theta&-\frac{1}{\lambda}\sin\theta\\
\frac{1}{\lambda}\sin\theta&\frac{1}{\lambda}\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\end{pmatrix}\sim g_{A}

The map
g_{A}
acts on the hyperbolic component of
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
as an elliptic isometry, it fixes exactly one point inside
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and the angle of rotation is given by
\theta=-2\log|\lambda|
. The isometry
g_{A}
is Shilov hyperbolic if and only if
|\lambda|\neq 1
.

The geometrical interpretation of the action of
g_{A}
on
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
in the hyperbolic, parabolic and elliptic case is illustrated in Figure
34
.

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 34.
The action of
g_{A}
on
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
in the hyperbolic (with
\det A>0
), parabolic and elliptic case

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
g
be an element of
{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
fixing two Lagrangians
l_{1},l_{2}
in
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{4})^{(2)}
i.e.
g
is conjugated to
g_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&A^{-T}\end{pmatrix}
for a matrix
A\in{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Denote by
|\lambda|\geq|\mu|
the modulus of the eigenvalues of
A
. Then

(i)

There exists
l\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
such that
(l_{1},l,l_{2})
maximal and
(l_{1},l,g(l),l_{2})
maximal if and only if
|\mu|>1
(that is
g
is Shilov hyperbolic).

(ii)

(l_{1},l,g(l),l_{2})
maximal for all
l
such that
(l_{1},l,l_{2})
maximal if and only if
A=\lambda{\rm Id}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\ \lambda>1
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the lemma for
g=g_{A}
, that is
l_{1}=0
and
l_{2}=\infty
.

(i)

We want to show that there exists
Y
such that
(0,Y,\infty)
maximal and
(0,Y,g_{A}Y,\infty)
maximal if and only if
|\mu|>1
. Let us write
Y>0
for a matrix
Y
which is positive definite. By Lemma
2.2
we know that
(0,Y,\infty)
is maximal if and only if
Y>0
. Suppose there exists
Y>0
such that
(0,Y,g_{A}Y,\infty)
maximal, that is

g_{A}Y-Y
=
AYA^{T}-Y>0
. Recall that a matrix
M
is positive definite if and only if
NMN^{T}>0
for every invertible matrix
N
. In particular for
N=\sqrt{Y^{-1}}
we obtain

(19)

\sqrt{Y^{-1}}(AYA^{T}-Y)\sqrt{Y^{-1}}=(\sqrt{Y^{-1}}A\sqrt{Y})(\sqrt{Y}A^{T}\sqrt{Y^{-1}})-{\rm Id}>0

The matrix
\sqrt{Y}A^{T}\sqrt{Y^{-1}}\in{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
has the same eigenvalues of
A
. Let
v
be the orthonormal eigenvector associated to
\mu
. Then
\sqrt{Y}A^{T}\sqrt{Y^{-1}}v=\mu v
and we deduce
\sqrt{Y}A^{T}\sqrt{Y^{-1}}\overline{v}=\overline{\mu}\overline{v}
. It follows from (
19
):

v^{T}(\sqrt{Y^{-1}}A\sqrt{Y})(\sqrt{Y}A^{T}\sqrt{Y^{-1}})\overline{v}-v^{T}\overline{v}=|\mu|^{2}-1>0

Suppose
|\mu|>1
. We want to find
Y>0
such that
(0,Y,g_{A}Y,\infty)
maximal that is we want to find
Y>0
such that
g_{A}Y-Y>0
. In Remark
2.23
we have given an equivalent condition for
g_{A}Y-Y
to be positive definite: let
\mathbf{r}=d^{\mathbb{R}}(\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(iY),\pi^{\mathbb{R}}(ig_{A}Y))
and
\mathbf{h}=d^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(iY),\pi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(ig_{A}Y))
, then

(20)

g_{A}Y-Y>0\iff\mathbf{r}>\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbf{h}

As
g_{A}
Shilov hyperbolic (this is the assumption
|\mu|<1
) we know that
g_{A}
acts as a translation of distance
\mathbf{r}
on the
\mathbb{R}
-component of
\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty}
and as an isometry on the
\mathbb{H}^{2}
-component which can be hyperbolic parabolic or elliptic (see Example
7.2
). Observe that for a fixed
A
the distance
\mathbf{r}
only depends on the eigenvalues of
A
and not on the point
Y
, whereas the distance
\mathbf{h}
depends on
Y
and decreases the more
Y
is close to the axis (if the isometry is hyperbolic) or to a fixed point (if the isometry is parabolic or elliptic). We can always find an open neighbour of the axis (or of a fixed point) such that the condition
\mathbf{r}>\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbf{h}
is satisfied.

(ii)

We want to show that
(0,Y,g_{A}Y,\infty)
is maximal
\forall Y
such that
(0,Y,\infty)
maximal if and only if
A=\lambda{\rm Id}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\ \lambda>1
. This is clear after the discussion in
(i)
: recall that
\lambda{\rm Id}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
acts on the
\mathbb{H}^{2}
-component of the tube as the identity map, so if
A=\lambda{\rm Id}
the inequality in (
20
) is clearly satisfied. Conversely, suppose that
(0,Y,g_{A}Y,\infty)
is maximal for all
Y>0
. Equivalently, for any
Y>0
the inequality in (
20
) is satisfied, where the distance
\mathbf{r}
is a fixed length depending only on the eigenvalues of
A
. This implies that the action of
g_{A}
on the hyperbolic component of the tube is the identity map i.e.
g_{A}=\lambda{\rm Id},\ \lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. If this were not the case, we could always find an
Y
which does not satisfy the inequality in (
20
) by stepping away from the axis (in the hyperbolic case) or the fixed points (in the parabolic or elliptic case) and moving towards the boundary of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

∎

7.2.
Maximal representations

Let
\Sigma
be an oriented surface with negative Euler characteristic and boundary
\partial\Sigma
. Fix a finite area hyperbolization on
\Sigma
inducing an action of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
on
S^{1}=\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
. An element
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
is called
peripheral
if it is freely homotopic to a boundary component. Maximal representations are representations that maximize the Toledo invariant, an invariant defined using bounded cohomology (see
[
Tol89
]
,
[
BIW10
]
). It is a deep result from Burger Iozzi and Wienhard (
[
BIW10
, Theorem 8]
) that maximal representations can
be equivalently characterized as representations admitting a well-behaved boundary map.

Definition 7.4
.

A representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
is
maximal
if there exists a
\rho
-equivariant map
\xi:S^{1}\to\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
which is monotone (i.e. the image of any positively oriented triple in the circle is a maximal triple) and right continuous.

Given a maximal representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, the image
\rho(\gamma)
of every non-peripheral element
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
is Shilov hyperbolic (see
[
Str15
]
). Equivalently,
\rho(\gamma)
fixes two transverse Lagrangians
l_{\gamma}^{+}
and
l_{\gamma}^{-}
on which it acts expandingly and contractingly respectively. These Lagrangians are the images
\xi(\gamma^{+})
and
\xi(\gamma^{-})
where
\xi:S^{1}\to\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
is the equivariant boundary map and
l^{\pm}_{\gamma}=\xi(\gamma^{\pm})
.
We want to parametrize the set of maximal representations where the property of being Shilov hyperbolic is true also for peripheral elements. This is equivalent to the requirement that the representations are Anosov in the sense of
[
GW12
]
.

Definition 7.5
.

A maximal representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
will be called
Shilov hyperbolic
if
\rho(\gamma)
is Shilov hyperbolic for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. The set of maximal representations which are Shilov hyperbolic will be denoted by
\text{Hom}^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R}))
. We define
\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R}))
the quotient

\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})):=\text{Hom}^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R}))/_{{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})}

where
{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
is acting by conjugation:
\rho\sim\rho^{\prime}
if there exists
g\in{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
such that
\rho(\gamma)=g\rho^{\prime}(\gamma)g^{-1}
for all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.

Notation 7.6
.

For the rest of the paper we wll denote by
W_{3}
the reflection group

\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}=\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}|\ s_{1}^{2}=s_{2}^{2}=s_{3}^{2}=1\rangle
.

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 35.
The reflections
\rho(s_{1}),\rho(s_{2}),\rho(s_{3})
for
\rho:W_{3}\to{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(2n,\mathbb{R})
maximal

Definition 7.7
.

A representation
\rho:W_{3}\to{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(2n,\mathbb{R})
is
maximal
if there exists a maximal 6-tuple of Lagrangians
(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
such that
\rho(s_{1}),\rho(s_{2}),\rho(s_{3})
are reflections of
\mathcal{X}
fixing
(P_{1},P_{2}),(Q_{1},Q_{2}),(R_{1},R_{2})
respectively and such that

\begin{cases}\rho(s_{1})(X_{1})=X_{2}\text{ and }\rho(s_{1})(Z_{1})=Z_{2}\\
\rho(s_{2})(X_{1})=X_{2}\text{ and }\rho(s_{2})(Y_{1})=Y_{2}\\
\rho(s_{3})(Y_{1})=Y_{2}\text{ and }\rho(s_{3})(Z_{1})=Z_{2}\end{cases}

where
X_{1},X_{2},Y_{1},Y_{2},Z_{1},Z_{2}
are uniquely determined by

(21)

\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{X_{1},X_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Q_{1},Q_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Y_{1},Y_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{R_{1},R_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Z_{1},Z_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}}

The space of maximal representations will be denoted by
\text{Hom}^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(2n,\mathbb{R}))
. We further define

\chi^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(2n,\mathbb{R})):=\text{Hom}^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(2n,\mathbb{R}))/_{{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})}

Remark 7.8
.

Given the maximal 6-tuple
(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
the set of reflections
\rho(s_{i}),i=1,2,3
for which
\rho:W_{3}\to{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})^{\pm}
is maximal as in Definition
7.7
are given by the sets
\mathcal{R}(P_{1},Z_{2},X_{1},P_{2}),\mathcal{R}(Q_{1},X_{2},Y_{1},Q_{2})
and
\mathcal{R}(R_{1},Y_{2},Z_{1},R_{2})
respectively.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
W_{3}=\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}|\ s_{1}^{2}=s_{2}^{2}=s_{3}^{2}=1\rangle
and let
\rho:W_{3}\to{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})^{\pm}
be maximal. Then the composition
\rho(s_{i}s_{j})=\rho(s_{i})\rho(s_{j})
is a Shilov hyperbolic element of
{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
for any
i\neq j
where
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Proof.

The product of any two reflections is an element of
{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
: for two reflections
\rho(s_{i})=R_{i},\rho(s_{j})=R_{j}
it holds

(R_{i}R_{j})^{T}J(R_{i}R_{j})=R_{j}^{T}R_{i}^{T}JR_{i}R_{j}=R_{j}^{T}(-J)R_{j}=J

Let
\rho:W_{3}\to{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})^{\pm}
be maximal, we want to show that
\rho(s_{i})\rho(s_{j})
is Shilov hyperbolic. Without loss of generality let us assume
i=1
,
j=2
. By definition of maximality it is clear that
\rho(s_{1})\rho(s_{2})
fixes
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, where
(P_{1},X_{1},P_{2},Q_{1},X_{2},Q_{2})
is a maximal 6-tuple and
\mathcal{Y}_{P_{1},P_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{X_{1},X_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Q_{1},Q_{2}}
. Up to isometry let us consider
(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
to be
(0,A,{\rm Id},C,D,\infty)
where
A,C,D
are positive definite and
C
is diagonal. The map
\rho(s_{1})\rho(s_{2})
is inside
{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
and fixes
0
and
\infty
. This map is Shilov hyperbolic if and only if there exists a positive definite
Y
such that
(0,Y,\rho(s_{1})\rho(s_{2})Y,\infty)
is maximal (see Lemma
7.3
). Let
Y=A
. Then
\rho(s_{1})\rho(s_{2})(A)=\rho(s_{1})(A)
. We want to show that
(0,A,\rho(s_{1})A,\infty)
is maximal. We know
(0,A,C)
maximal and
\rho(s_{1})\in\mathcal{R}(-D,0,C,D)
(Remark
7.8
). Result follows by Proposition
6.17
and
2.3
.
∎

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
\rho:W_{3}\to{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R})
be maximal and let
(X_{1},X_{2},Y_{1},Y_{2},Z_{1},Z_{2})
be a maximal 6-tuple as in Definition
7.7
.
Then for
l_{1},l_{2},l_{3},l_{4}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
it holds:

(i)

If
(X_{2},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3},l_{4},Z_{1})
is maximal then
(Z_{2},\rho(s_{1})l_{4},\rho(s_{1})l_{3},\rho(s_{1})l_{2},\rho(s_{1})l_{1},X_{1})
is maximal

(ii)

If
(Y_{2},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3},l_{4},X_{1})
is maximal then
(X_{2},\rho(s_{2})l_{4},\rho(s_{2})l_{3},\rho(s_{2})l_{2},\rho(s_{2})l_{1},Y_{1})
is maximal

(iii)

If
(Z_{2},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3},l_{4},Y_{1})
is maximal then
(Y_{2},\rho(s_{3})l_{4},\rho(s_{3})l_{3},\rho(s_{3})l_{2},\rho(s_{3})l_{1},Z_{1})
is maximal

Proof.

Follows directly from Proposition
6.17
.
∎

7.3.
Arc coordinates in classical Teichmüller

Given a hyperbolic surface with boundary, arc coordinates provide a parametrization of the Teichmüller space.

Given
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,m}
a compact orientable smooth surface of genus
g
and
m
boundary components, we can equip
\Sigma_{g,m}
with a complete hyperbolic structure of finite volume with geodesic boundary. The universal covering
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{g,m}
of
\Sigma_{g,m}
is a closed subset of the hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}
where boundary curves are geodesics. Let
\{a_{1},...,a_{k}\}
be a maximal collection of pairwise disjoint arcs in
\Sigma_{g,m}
with starting and ending point on a boundary component which are essential and pairwise non-homotopic. The connected components of
\Sigma_{g,m}\setminus\bigcup_{i}a_{i}
are given by a union of hexagons. Every arc will be called an
edge
of the hexagon decomposition. For every hexagon there are exactly three alternating edges belonging to one boundary component of
\Sigma_{g,m}
. We denote by
E
the set of all edges,
E_{bdry}
the set of edges lying on a boundary component and by
\mathcal{H}
the set of all hexagons of the decomposition. It can be shown that for such a collection
\{a_{1},...,a_{k}\}
it holds

k=\#E\backslash E_{bdry}=3|\chi(\Sigma_{g,m})|=3(2g-2+m)

and that the number of hexagons is given by
2|\chi(\Sigma_{g,m})|=2(2g-2+m)
. For a fixed hyperbolic structure we can always realize the hexagon decomposition of
\Sigma_{g,m}
in a way such that every edge is a geodesic and every arc
a_{i}\in\{a_{1},...,a_{k}\}
is the unique geodesic which is orthogonal to the boundary at both endpoints. We fix an orientation on the boundary components such that the surface lies to the right of the boundary.
For each choice of
\{a_{1},...,a_{k}\}
we get a parametrization of the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma_{g,m})
: once we fix the lengths
l(a_{1}),...,l(a_{k})
there is a unique hyperbolic metric that makes
\Sigma_{g,m}\setminus\bigcup_{i}a_{i}
a union of hyperbolic right-angled hexagons where each hexagon has exactly three alternating edges
a_{i_{1}},a_{i_{2}},a_{i_{3}}
in
E\backslash E_{bdry}
of length
l(a_{i_{1}}),l(a_{i_{2}}),l(a_{i_{3}})
respectively, where
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\in\{1,...,k\}
. This is due to the well known fact that given three real numbers
b,c,d>0
there exists (up to isometries) a unique right-angled hexagon in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
with alternating sides of lengths
b,c
and
d
.

Let us denote by
\Gamma_{g,m}
the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,m})
. It is well known that
\Gamma_{g,m}
is isomorphic to the free group
\mathbb{F}_{2g+m-1}
and that one can define the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma_{g,m})
as the set of conjugacy classes of discrete and faithful representations
\rho:\Gamma_{g,m}\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.

In Definition
7.5
we have defined the space
\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{g,m},{\rm PSp}(2n,\mathbb{R}))
. When
n=1
the group
{\rm PSp}(2,\mathbb{R})
coincides with
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and a Shilov hyperbolic element in
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is conjugated to a matrix of the type
\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\lambda^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
where
|\lambda|>1
. A representation
\rho
in
{\rm Hom}(\Gamma_{g,m},{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
is discrete and faithful if and only if
\rho
is maximal. The surface
\Sigma_{g,m}
is then realized by the quotient
\Sigma_{g,m}=_{\rho(\Gamma_{g,m})}\backslash\mathbb{H}^{2}
where
\rho(\Gamma_{g,m})
acts freely and properly discontinuously on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Restricting to Shilov hyperbolic representations is equivalent to require that the surface
\Sigma_{g,m}
does not have cusps as boundary components. The above discussion asserts that once we fix the lengths
l(\alpha_{1}),...,l(\alpha_{k})
we can explicitly write the representation
\rho:\Gamma_{g,m}\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
in
{\rm Hom}^{\text{max},\text{Shilov}}(\Gamma_{g,m},{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
such that
\Sigma_{g,m}=\rho(\Gamma_{g,m})\backslash\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

Example 7.11
.

Let
\Gamma_{0,3}
be the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
, which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. We denote
\Gamma_{0,3}=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
and consider
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
three arcs as in Figure
1
which decompose
\Sigma_{0,3}
in two hexagons. Once we fix the lengths
l(a_{1}),l(a_{2}),l(a_{3})
we can uniquely draw two adjacent isometric hexagons in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
up to isometry and we can reconstruct the generators
\rho(\alpha),\rho(\beta)
of the maximal representation which "closes up" the pair of pants. These are two hyperbolic isometries inside
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.

7.4.
The group
\Gamma_{0,3}
as a subgroup of
W_{3}

The fundamental group
\Gamma_{0,3}
is isomorphic to the free group
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. On the other hand, the group
W_{3}=\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}|\ s_{1}^{2}=s_{2}^{2}=s_{3}^{2}=1\rangle

has a normal subgroup
\Gamma=\langle s_{1}s_{2},s_{2}s_{3}\rangle
isomorphic to the free group
\mathbb{F}_{2}
.
This allows us to see
\Gamma_{0,3}
as a subgroup of
W_{3}
.

Proposition 7.12
.

Let
\Gamma_{0,3}=\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
and
W_{3}=\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}|\ s_{1}^{2}=s_{2}^{2}=s_{3}^{2}=1\rangle
. Fix
\widetilde{\rho}\in{\rm Hom}^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSL}^{\pm}(2,\mathbb{R}))
. Denote by
\phi
the following homomorphism

\displaystyle\phi:\Gamma_{0,3}

\displaystyle\to W_{3}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{1}s_{2}

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{2}s_{3}

It holds:

(i)

The representation
\rho:=\widetilde{\rho}|_{Im(\phi)}
is inside
{\rm Hom}^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
.

(ii)

For any
\rho\in{\rm Hom}^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
there exists a unique
\widetilde{\rho}\in{\rm Hom}^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSL}^{\pm}(2,\mathbb{R}))
such that
\rho=\widetilde{\rho}\circ\phi

(iii)

The following map
f
is a homeomorphism:

\displaystyle f:\chi^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSL}^{\pm}(2,\mathbb{R}))

\displaystyle\to\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}))

\displaystyle\bigl[\widetilde{\rho}\bigr]

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl[\widetilde{\rho}|_{Im(\phi)}\bigr]

Proof.

(i)

This will be proven for
{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
in Proposition
7.14
. The proof for
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is similar.

(ii)

Let
\rho\in{\rm Hom}^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
. Denote by
\{(x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2}),(z_{1},z_{2})\}\subset\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
the fixed points of
\rho(\alpha),\rho(\beta)
and
\rho(\beta^{-1}\alpha^{-1})
respectively. Choose an orientation of the boundary
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
such that
(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},z_{1},z_{2})
is positive. In Section
6.1
we have defined
a reflection in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
as an involution of
{\rm SL}^{-}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Reflections in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
fix an infinite geodesic
\gamma
and are uniquely determined by the endpoints of
\gamma
at the boundary of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. For
p,q\in\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
let
\gamma_{p,q}
denote the infinite geodesic having
p,q
as endpoints.

Notation 7.13
.

For
p,q\in\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
we denote
R_{p,q}
the unique non trivial reflection fixing the infinite geodesic
\gamma_{p,q}
. In other words
R_{p,q}
is the unique non trivial isometry such that

\gamma_{p,q}\perp\gamma_{x,R_{p,q}(x)}

for any
x\in\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

Let
(p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2},r_{1},r_{2})
be the positive 6-tuple inside
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
uniquely determined by

\gamma_{x_{1},x_{2}}\perp\gamma_{p_{1},p_{2}}\perp\gamma_{y_{1},y_{2}}\perp\gamma_{q_{1},q_{2}}\perp\gamma_{z_{1},z_{2}}\perp\gamma_{r_{1},r_{2}}\perp\gamma_{x_{1},x_{2}}

Define
\widetilde{\rho}:W_{3}\to{\rm PSL}^{\pm}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that (Figure
36
)

\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})=R_{r_{1},r_{2}},\ \ \widetilde{\rho}(s_{2})=R_{p_{1},p_{2}},\ \ \widetilde{\rho}(s_{3})=R_{q_{1},q_{2}}

[formula]
[formula]
)
[formula]
ρ(β)
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 36.
The maximal representation
\rho
as a restriction of
\widetilde{\rho}

Then
\widetilde{\rho}
is maximal.
Moreover it is easy to show that (see for example
[
Mar16
]
Proposition 6.2.1)
\rho(\alpha)=R_{q_{1},q_{2}}\circ R_{p_{1},p_{2}}
and

\rho(\beta)=R_{r_{1},r_{2}}\circ R_{q_{1},q_{2}}
. It follows

\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1}s_{2})=\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})\widetilde{\rho}(s_{2})=\rho(\alpha)\text{ and }\widetilde{\rho}(s_{2}s_{3})=\widetilde{\rho}(s_{2})\widetilde{\rho}(s_{3})=\rho(\beta)

so that
\widetilde{\rho}\circ\phi(\gamma)=\rho(\gamma)
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma_{0,3}
. It is clear that
\widetilde{\rho}
is the unique maximal representation such that
\widetilde{\rho}\circ\phi=\rho
.

(iii)

This follows directly from
(ii)
. In particular recall that as
\Gamma_{0,3}
is free, the set of representations
{\rm Hom}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
can be identified with
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})^{2}
and we can carry over its topology.

∎

7.5.
The set
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}

In Definition
7.7
we have defined the set of maximal representations
{\rm Hom}^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}))
and we know that we can see the fundamental group
\Gamma_{0,3}
as a subgroup of
W_{3}
. In this section we define the set
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}\subset\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
. We start by giving an analogue of Proposition
7.12
(i)
in the case of
{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
, that is we show that the restriction to
\Gamma_{0,3}
of a maximal representation as in Definition
7.7
is a maximal and Shilov hyperbolic representation as in Definition
7.5
.

Proposition 7.14
.

Let
\Gamma_{0,3}=\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
and
W_{3}=\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}|\ s_{1}^{2}=s_{2}^{2}=s_{3}^{2}=1\rangle
. Fix
\widetilde{\rho}\in{\rm Hom}^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}))
. Denote by
\phi
the following homomorphism

\displaystyle\phi:\Gamma_{0,3}

\displaystyle\to W_{3}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{1}s_{2}

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{2}s_{3}

Then the representation
\rho:=\widetilde{\rho}|_{Im(\phi)}
is inside
{\rm Hom}^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
.

Proof.

It is not hard to show that
\widetilde{\rho}\circ\phi(\gamma)
is an element of
{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma_{0,3}
. By abuse of notation let us denote the subgroup
\phi(\Gamma_{0,3})\trianglelefteq W_{3}
just as
\Gamma_{0,3}
.
Given
\widetilde{\rho}
in
{\rm Hom}^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}))
we want to prove that
\rho=\widetilde{\rho}\circ\phi:\Gamma_{0,3}\to{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
is maximal. As
\widetilde{\rho}:W_{3}\to{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R})
is maximal we know that there exists a maximal 6-tuple
(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
satisfying the conditions of Definition
7.7
. In particular
(P_{1},P_{2})
,
(Q_{1},Q_{2})
and
(R_{1},R_{2})
are the fixpoints of
\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1}),\widetilde{\rho}(s_{2})
and
\widetilde{\rho}(s_{3})
respectively and let us denote
(X_{1},X_{2},Y_{1},Y_{2},Z_{1},Z_{2})
the Lagrangians satisfying (
21
). We obtain the representation
\rho
where

\begin{cases}\rho(\alpha)=\widetilde{\rho}\circ\phi(s_{1}s_{2})\text{ fixes }X_{1},X_{2}\\
\rho(\beta)=\widetilde{\rho}\circ\phi(s_{2}s_{3})\text{ fixes }Y_{1},Y_{2}\\
\rho(\beta^{-1}\alpha^{-1})=\widetilde{\rho}\circ\phi(s_{3}s_{1})\text{ fixes }Z_{1},Z_{2}\end{cases}

To prove maximality of
\rho
we use
[
Str15
, Corollary 3.4.2.]
, which gives a sufficient condition for a representation to be maximal by computing the Maslov index of elements in the Shilov boundary fixed by
\rho(C_{i})
, where
C_{i}
are the peripheral elements of
\Gamma_{0,3}
. Finally, we know by Lemma
7.9
that
\widetilde{\rho}(s_{i},s_{j})
is Shilov hyperbolic for any
i\neq j
. Any maximal representation sends non peripheral elements to Shilov hyperbolic elements in
{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
(
[
Str15
]
).

Additionally, we provide an explicit construction of the boundary map
\xi:S^{1}\to\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
in the spirit of
[
FG06
]
. Fix
\rho_{0}
a hyperbolization of
\Sigma_{0,3}
.
Denote
\{(x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2}),(z_{1},z_{2})\}
the fixed points of
\rho_{0}(\alpha),\rho_{0}(\beta)
and
\rho_{0}(\beta^{-1}\alpha^{-1})
respectively. Choose an orientation of the boundary
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
such that
(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},z_{1},z_{2})
is positive. By Proposition
7.12
(ii)
we know that there is a unique way to extend the action of
\Gamma_{0,3}
on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
(and on its boundary) to the group
W_{3}
such that
\rho_{0}=\widetilde{\rho}_{0}\circ\phi
where
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}:W_{3}\to{\rm PSL}^{\pm}(2,\mathbb{R})
is maximal. For simplicity given
s\in W_{3}
and
p\in\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
we will denote the action
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}(s)\cdot p
simply as
s\cdot p
.

As
\widetilde{\rho}:W_{3}\to{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R})
is maximal there exists a maximal 6-tuple
(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
satisfying the conditions of Definition
7.7
. Let us denote
(X_{1},X_{2},Y_{1},Y_{2},Z_{1},Z_{2})
the Lagrangians satisfying (
21
). We define the following sets

H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}:=\{x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},z_{1},z_{2}\}

H^{\mathcal{L}}:=\{X_{1},X_{2},Y_{1},Y_{2},Z_{1},Z_{2}\}

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n}:=\bigcup_{|s|\leq n}s\cdot H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}},\ s\in W_{3}

\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{n}:=\bigcup_{|s|\leq n}\widetilde{\rho}(s)\cdot H^{\mathcal{L}}

Define
\xi_{n}:\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n}\to\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
as

\begin{cases}\Big(\xi_{n}(x_{1}),\xi_{n}(x_{2}),\xi_{n}(y_{1}),\xi_{n}(y_{2}),\xi_{n}(z_{1}),\xi_{n}(z_{2})\Big)=(X_{1},X_{2},Y_{1},Y_{2},Z_{1},Z_{2})\\
\xi_{n}(s\cdot p)=\widetilde{\rho}(s)\xi_{n}(p)\text{ for }s\in W_{3},|s|\leq n,\ p\in H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\end{cases}

We show that the map
\xi_{n}
is monotone by induction on
n
.

\bm{n=0}
: From the definition of
\xi
it is clear that the map
\xi_{0}:H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\to H^{\mathcal{L}}
is monotone.

\bm{n=1}
: We obtain the map
\xi_{1}:\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{1}\to\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
where

\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}=H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\cup\{s_{1}H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}},s_{2}H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}},s_{3}H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\}

\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathcal{L}}=H^{\mathcal{L}}\cup\{\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})H^{\mathcal{L}},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{2})H^{\mathcal{L}},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{3})H^{\mathcal{L}}\}

The set
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
is given by
H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
together with other six points, two for every
s_{i}H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}},i\in\{1,2,3\}
. For
s_{1}H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
we only add the two points {
s_{1}y_{1},s_{1}y_{2}
} as
s_{1}x_{1}=x_{2}
,

and
s_{1}z_{1}=z_{2}
,
s_{1}z_{2}=z_{1}
. The same holds for
s_{2}H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
and

. This is illustrated in Figure

.

The set
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
is therefore formed by 12 points. The order on

. To explicitly write

to show that the quadruples

and

are positive. We obtain the following positive 12-tuple:

\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}=(z_{2},s_{1}y_{2},s_{1}y_{1},x_{1},x_{2},s_{2}z_{2},s_{2}z_{1},y_{1},y_{2},s_{3}x_{2},s_{3}x_{1},z_{1})

Similarly, the set
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathcal{L}}
consists of 12 Lagrangians: it is given by
H^{\mathcal{L}}
together with six Lagrangians, two for every
\widetilde{\rho}(s_{i})H^{\mathcal{L}}
. For
\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})H^{\mathcal{L}}
we only add the Lagrangians
\{\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})Y_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})Y_{2}\}
. By maximality of

we know (

)

\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})X_{1}=X_{2},\ \widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})X_{2}=X_{1}\text{ and }\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})Z_{1}=Z_{2},\ \widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})Z_{2}=Z_{1}

and the same is true for

and

. To prove monotonic behaviour of

we need to show maximality of the 12-tuple given by

\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathcal{L}}=(Z_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})Y_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})Y_{1},X_{1},X_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{2})Z_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{2})Z_{1},Y_{1},Y_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{3})X_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{3})X_{1},Z_{1})

We use Lemma
7.10
to show that the three quadruples given by
\big(Z_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})Y_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})Y_{1},X_{1}\big)
,
\big(X_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{2})Z_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{2})Z_{1},Y_{1}\big)
, and
\big(Y_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{3})X_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{3})X_{1},Z_{1}\big)
are maximal . We use Lemma
2.3
to deduce that the 12-tuple is therefore maximal.

Assume true for

\bm{n}

show true for

\bm{n+1}
: Assuming
\xi_{n}
monotone we consider the map
\xi_{n+1}:\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n+1}\to\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{n+1}
. We first study the set
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n+1}
. We describe how to obtain it from
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n}
and how to write its positive order (
Claim 1
and
Claim 2
). The set
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n+1}
is given by

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n+1}=\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n}\cup\big\{s\cdot H,\ |s|=n+1\big\}

If we fix an element
s\in W_{3}
such that
|s|=n+1
and look at the set
\{s\cdot H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\}
we are adding exactly two points inside
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n+1}
both lying between two points contained in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n}
. This is made precise in the following two statements:

Claim 1
: For any
s\in W_{3}
such that
|s|=n+1
it holds
|\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n}\cup\{s\cdot H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\}|=|\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n}|+2
.

Claim 2
: Let
s=ws_{i}
where
|w|=n
and
s_{i}\in W_{3}
. It holds

(1)

If
s_{i}=s_{1}
then the two points
ws_{1}\cdot H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
added inside
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n+1}
are
\{ws_{1}y_{1},ws_{1}y_{2}\}
where

\begin{cases}(wz_{2},ws_{1}y_{2},ws_{1}y_{1},wx_{1})\text{ positive if }n\text{ even}\\
(wx_{1},ws_{1}y_{1},ws_{1}y_{2},wz_{2})\text{ positive if }n\text{ odd}\end{cases}

(2)

If
s_{i}=s_{2}
then the two points
ws_{2}\cdot H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
added inside
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n+1}
are
\{ws_{2}z_{1},ws_{2}z_{2}\}
where

\begin{cases}(wx_{2},ws_{2}z_{2},ws_{2}z_{1},wy_{1})\text{ positive if }n\text{ even}\\
(wy_{1},ws_{2}z_{1},ws_{2}z_{2},wx_{2})\text{ positive if }n\text{ odd}\end{cases}

(3)

If
s_{i}=s_{3}
then the two points
ws_{3}\cdot H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
added inside
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n+1}
are
\{ws_{3}x_{1},ws_{3}x_{2}\}
where

\begin{cases}(wy_{2},ws_{3}x_{2},ws_{3}x_{1},wz_{1})\text{ positive if }n\text{ even}\\
(wz_{1},ws_{3}x_{1},ws_{3}x_{2},wy_{2})\text{ positive if }n\text{ odd}\end{cases}

Proof of Claim 1
Let
s\in W_{3}
such that
|s|=n+1
and consider the set
s\cdot H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
. Suppose
s
ends with the element
s_{1}
i.e. we can write

s=ws_{1}
for a
w\in W_{3}
,
|w|=n
. Among the six points
ws_{1}\cdot H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}=\{ws_{1}x_{1},ws_{1}x_{2},ws_{1}y_{1},ws_{1}y_{2},ws_{1}z_{1},ws_{1}z_{2}\}
we know

ws_{1}x_{1}=wx_{2},\ ws_{1}x_{2}=wx_{1}\text{ and }ws_{1}z_{1}=wz_{2},\ ws_{1}z_{2}=wz_{1}

so that
\{ws_{1}x_{1},ws_{1}x_{2},ws_{1}z_{1},ws_{1}z_{2}\}\subset\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n}
. In particular

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n}\cup\{ws_{1}\cdot H^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\}=\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n}\cup\{ws_{1}y_{1},ws_{2}y_{2}\}

A similar proof holds for
s=ws_{2}
and
s=ws_{3}
.
\square

Proof of Claim 2
Let us show (1) In the proof of
Claim 1
we have already shown that the two points added inside
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n+1}
are
\{ws_{1}y_{1},ws_{1}y_{2}\}
. We know that we can write
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
as the positive 12-tuple (inductive step
n=1
)

\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}=(z_{2},s_{1}y_{2},s_{1}y_{1},x_{1},x_{2},s_{2}z_{2},s_{2}z_{1},y_{1},y_{2},s_{3}x_{2},s_{3}x_{1},z_{1})

In particular
(z_{2},s_{1}y_{2},s_{1}y_{1},x_{1})
is positive. Let
w=w_{n}\cdot...\cdot w_{1}
, where
w_{i}\in\{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\}
. At every step

\displaystyle(w_{1}z_{2},w_{1}s_{1}y_{2},w_{1}s_{1}y_{1},w_{1}x_{1})\to(w_{1}w_{2}z_{2},w_{1}w_{2}s_{1}y_{2},w_{1}w_{2}s_{1}y_{1},w_{1}w_{2}x_{1})\to...

\displaystyle...\to(w_{1}w_{2}...w_{n}z_{2},w_{1}w_{2}...w_{n}s_{1}y_{2},w_{1}w_{2}...w_{n}s_{1}y_{1},w_{1}w_{2}...w_{n}x_{1})

we satisfy the conditions of Proposition
6.7
. It follows that the image under

of the positive quadruple

stays positive if
n
even and is negative if
n
odd. Points (2) and (3) are similar.
\square

We state similar statements for the set
\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{n+1}
.

Claim 3
: For any
s\in W_{3}
such that
|s|=n+1
it holds
|\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\cup\{\widetilde{\rho}(s)\cdot H^{\mathcal{L}}\}|=|\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{n}|+2
.

(1)

If

are

\begin{cases}(\widetilde{\rho}(w)Z_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{1})Y_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{1})Y_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(w)X_{1})\text{ maximal if }n\text{ even}\\
(\widetilde{\rho}(w)X_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{1})Y_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{1})Y_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(w)Z_{2})\text{ maximal if }n\text{ odd}\end{cases}

(2)

If
s_{i}=s_{2}
then the two Lagrangians
\rho(ws_{2})\cdot H^{\mathcal{L}}
added inside
\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{n+1}
are
\{\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{2})Z_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{2})Z_{2}\}

\begin{cases}(\widetilde{\rho}(w)X_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{2})Z_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{2})Z_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(w)Y_{1})\text{ maximal if }n\text{ even}\\
(\widetilde{\rho}(w)Y_{1}),\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{2})Z_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{2})Z_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(w)X_{2})\text{ maximal if }n\text{ odd}\end{cases}

(3)

If
s_{i}=s_{3}
then the two Lagrangians
\rho(ws_{3})\cdot H^{\mathcal{L}}
added inside
\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{n+1}
are
\{\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{3})X_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{3})X_{2}\}
where

\begin{cases}(\widetilde{\rho}(w)Y_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{3})X_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{3})X_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(w)Z_{1})\text{ maximal if }n\text{ even}\\
(\widetilde{\rho}(w)Z_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{3})X_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{3})X_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(w)Y_{2})\text{ maximal if }n\text{ odd}\end{cases}

Proof of Claim 3
The proof is similar to
Claim 1
where we change
s
with
\widetilde{\rho}(s)
and
x_{i},y_{i},z_{i}
with

and follows directly from the definition of

).

Proof of Claim 4
Let us show (1). By definition of
\widetilde{\rho}
it is clear that the two Lagrangians added inside
\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{n+1}
are
\{\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{1})Y_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(ws_{1})Y_{2}\}
. We know that
(Z_{2},\rho(s_{1})Y_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1})Y_{1},X_{1})
is maximal (inductive step
n=1
). Let
w=w_{n}\cdot...\cdot w_{1}
, where

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\rho}(w_{1})Z_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(w_{1}s_{1})Y_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(w_{1}s_{1})Y_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(w_{1})X_{1})\to(\widetilde{\rho}(w_{1}w_{2})Z_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(w_{1}w_{2}s_{1})Y_{2},\widetilde{\rho}(w_{1}w_{2}s_{1})Y_{1},\widetilde{\rho}(w_{1}w_{2})X_{1})\to...

6.17
. It follows that the image under

of the maximal quadruple

is maximal if

is even and is minimal if

The map
\xi_{n}:\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n+1}\to\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
is monotone by inductive hypothesis. In
Claim 3
and
Claim 4
we have proven that the set
\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{n+1}
is obtained in the following way: for any

of length

we add two Lagrangians

in a way such that
(a,l_{1},l_{2},b)
maximal for

. By Lemma
2.3
it is easy to see that
\xi_{n+1}
is monotone on the entire set
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n+1}
.

We have proven that
\xi_{n}:\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{n+1}\to\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
is monotone for any
n\geq 0
and it is
\widetilde{\rho}
-equivariant by definition. In particular when we restrict the map to
\Gamma_{0,3}\leq W_{3}
, that is we consider the map
\rho=\widetilde{\rho}\circ\phi
, we obtain a monotone
\rho
-equivariant map.
Using the same approach of
[
BIW10
]
it can be shown that
\xi_{n}
can be extended to a map
\xi
defined on
S^{1}
with the same properties.
∎

Definition 7.15
.

We define
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}:=Im(f)
where
f
is the map

\displaystyle f:\chi^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}))

\displaystyle\to\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))

7.6.
Parameter space for
\chi^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}))

Let
\mathcal{X}
be the symmetric space associated to
{\rm Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
. Recall that we denote by

and

R_{st}=\begin{pmatrix}-{\rm Id}&0\\

\mathcal{K}=\Big\{\begin{pmatrix}-K&0\\
0&K\end{pmatrix},\ K\in{\rm PO}(2),\ K^{2}={\rm Id}\Big\}

Observe that
\{R_{st},R_{ex}\}\subset\mathcal{K}
. Recall also that we denote by

the malefic map (

).

.

\chi^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}))

such that

\underline{b}\in\mathfrak{a}\Rightarrow R_{2}\in\{R_{st},R_{ex}\}\\
F_{\underline{b},\underline{d},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\underline{c})\in\mathfrak{a}\Rightarrow R_{3}\in\{R_{st},R_{ex}\}\end{cases}

Configuration of hexagons
H
,
\overline{R}_{1}(H)

Proof.

From parameters to representations
:
Let
\big(\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d},[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}],R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}\big)
be in
\mathcal{S}
. Let
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})=(0,A,{\rm Id},C,D,\infty)
be a right-angled hexagon with
\mathcal{A}(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})=(\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d},[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}])
. In particular the maximal 12-tuple associated to
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty})
is given by

H=(\infty,-D,-A,0,A^{2},A,Z_{1},{\rm Id},C,Z_{2},D,DC^{-1}D)

where
Z_{1},Z_{2}
are uniquely determined by
\mathcal{Y}_{A^{2},{\rm Id}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{Z_{1},Z_{2}}\perp\mathcal{Y}_{C,DC^{-1}D}
. Let
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}
be isometries such that

\displaystyle g_{1}(D,DC^{-1}D,\infty,-D)=(0,{\rm Id},Y_{1},\infty)

\displaystyle g_{2}(-A,0,A^{2},A)=(0,{\rm Id},Y_{2},\infty)

\displaystyle g_{3}(Z_{1},{\rm Id},C,Z_{2})=(0,{\rm Id},Y_{3},\infty)

for
Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}
diagonal matrices inside
{\rm Sym}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Put

\overline{R}_{1}:=g_{1}^{-1}R_{1}g_{1}

\overline{R}_{2}:=g_{2}^{-1}R_{2}g_{2}

\overline{R}_{3}:=g_{3}^{-1}R_{3}g_{3}

By Corollary
6.20
we know that
\overline{R}_{1},\overline{R}_{2},\overline{R}_{3}
belong to
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}_{-D,D}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty},\mathcal{Y}_{C,DC^{-1}D}}
,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}_{-A,A}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{0,\infty},\mathcal{Y}_{A^{2},{\rm Id}}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}_{Z_{1},Z_{2}}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{A^{2},{\rm Id}},\mathcal{Y}_{C,DC^{-1}D}}
respectively. Let
W_{3}=\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}|\ s_{1}^{2}=s_{2}^{2}=s_{3}^{2}=1\rangle
and define
\widetilde{\rho}
as the representation

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}:W_{3}

\displaystyle\to{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle s_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\overline{R}_{1}

\displaystyle s_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto\overline{R}_{2}

\displaystyle s_{3}

\displaystyle\mapsto\overline{R}_{3}

The representation
\widetilde{\rho}
is maximal by construction. The images
\overline{R}_{i}(H)
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
are drawn in Figure
38
. The maps
g
and
h
appearing in the Figure are the generators of the representation restricted to the group
\Gamma_{0,3}
. This will be explained in Theorem
7.18
.

From representations to parameters
: Let
\widetilde{\rho}\in\chi^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}))
. We know that
\widetilde{\rho}
has the properties described in Definition
7.7
: we can determine a right-angled hexagon
(H,\mathcal{Y}_{X_{1},X_{2}})
where
X_{1},X_{2}
are as in Figure
35
. We compute the arc coordinates
\mathcal{A}(H,\mathcal{Y}_{X_{1},X_{2}})
. By definition we know that
\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1}),\widetilde{\rho}(s_{2})
and
\widetilde{\rho}(s_{3})
belong to the reflection sets of three alternating sides of this hexagon. We compute the corresponding elements in
\mathcal{K}^{3}
(one for every side) using Corollary
6.20
.
∎

7.7.
Parameter space for
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}

To parametrize
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
we impose an equivalent relation on
\mathcal{S}
identifying the points that have same image under
f
. Recall the set
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{A}
corresponds to right-angled hexagons in
\mathcal{X}
lying inside a maximal polydisk.

Definition 7.17
.

We define
\mathcal{S}_{0}\subset\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{K}^{3}
as the set

\mathcal{S}_{0}:=\Big\{(\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d},[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}],R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{K}^{3}|\ (\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d},[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}])\in\mathcal{D}\Big\}

We further define the following equivalent relation on
\mathcal{S}_{0}
:

(22)

(p,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\sim(p,R^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{3})\iff\begin{cases}R_{1}R_{2}=R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}\\
R_{2}R_{3}=R^{\prime}_{2}R^{\prime}_{3}\end{cases}

Theorem 7.18
.

The set
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
is parametrized by the parameter space
\mathcal{S}/_{\sim}
where

\mathcal{S}
consists of points
\big(\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d},[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}],R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}\big)
in
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{K}^{3}
such that

\begin{cases}\underline{d}\in\mathfrak{a}\Rightarrow R_{1}\in\{R_{st},R_{ex}\}\\
\underline{b}\in\mathfrak{a}\Rightarrow R_{2}\in\{R_{st},R_{ex}\}\\
F_{\underline{b},\underline{d},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\underline{c})\in\mathfrak{a}\Rightarrow R_{3}\in\{R_{st},R_{ex}\}\end{cases}

and if
\big(p,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}\big)\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
we put

(p,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\sim(p^{\prime},R^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{3})
where
\sim
is the equivalent relation in (
22
).

Proof.

From parameters to representations
:
Let
\big(\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d},[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}],R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}\big)
be in
\mathcal{S}
. The construction of a maximal
\widetilde{\rho}:W_{3}\to{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R})
is identical to the proof of Theorem
7.16
.
Let
\rho
be the restriction
\rho=f(\widetilde{\rho})
where
f
is the map of Definition
7.15
. Then
\rho
is inside
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
and we put

\big(\underline{b},\underline{c},\underline{d},[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}],R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}\big)=\rho\in\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
. In Figure
38
we have denoted

g=\widetilde{\rho}(s_{1}s_{2})
,
h=\widetilde{\rho}(s_{2}s_{3})
.

In the github repository
https://github.com/martamagnani/Arc-coord/blob/main/Param_for_chiS.py
we provide a Python program with output the maps
g,h
uniquely determining the maximal representation constructed above. We only provide the case
R_{i}\in\{R_{st},R_{ex}\}
.

The equivalence relation on
\mathcal{S}_{0}
:
Let
\Gamma_{0,3}=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
. A representation
\rho:\Gamma_{0,3}\to{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
is uniquely determined by the maps
\rho(\alpha)
,
\rho(\beta)
, which are exactly the maps
g
and
h
of Figure
38
. The isometry
g
is sending the hexagon
\overline{R}_{2}(H)
to
\overline{R}_{1}(H)
and the isometry
h
is sending the hexagon
\overline{R}_{3}(H)
to
\overline{R}_{2}(H)
.
The equivalence relation on
\mathcal{S}_{0}
identifies the points for which the map
f
of Definition
7.15
is not injective. More precisely for two points

s=(p,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
and
s^{\prime}=(p,R^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{3})
inside
S_{0}
we denote
\overline{R}_{i},\overline{R^{\prime}}_{i}
the reflections constructed from the parameters
s
and
s^{\prime}
respectively as shown in Figure
38
. In Remark
6.27
we have detected the points for which
\overline{R}_{2}(H)=\overline{R^{\prime}}_{2}(H)
, that is

\overline{R}_{2}=g_{2}^{-1}R_{st}g_{2},\ \overline{R^{\prime}}_{2}=g_{2}^{-1}R_{ex}g_{2}\ \ \text{ if }p\in\mathcal{D}\backslash\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}

where
H
and
\overline{R}_{2}(H)=\overline{R^{\prime}}_{2}(H)
are both contained in the model polydisk, and

\overline{R}_{2},\overline{R^{\prime}}_{2}\in g_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{K}g_{2}\ \ \text{ if }p\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}

where
H
and
\overline{R}_{2}(H)=\overline{R^{\prime}}_{2}(H)
are both contained in the diagonal disc. It is not hard to show a similar result for
\overline{R}_{i},\overline{R^{\prime}}_{i}
when
i\in\{1,3\}
.
Take two points
s,s^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{S}_{0}
such that for the corresponding reflections
\overline{R}_{i},\overline{R^{\prime}}_{i}
constructed in proof of Theorem
7.16
it holds

\begin{cases}\overline{R}_{1}(H)=\overline{R^{\prime}}_{1}(H)\\
\overline{R}_{2}(H)=\overline{R^{\prime}}_{2}(H)\\
\overline{R}_{3}(H)=\overline{R^{\prime}}_{3}(H)\end{cases}

The hexagons
H
,
\overline{R}_{i}(H)
,
\overline{R^{\prime}}_{i}(H)
of Figure
38
are all contained in the model polydisk. All the points of the polygonal chains drawn in Figure
33
are aligned. Then there exists exactly two maps
g,\widehat{g}
sending
\overline{R}_{2}(H)
to
\overline{R}_{1}(H)
and two maps
h,\widehat{h}
sending
\overline{R}_{3}(H)
to
\overline{R}_{2}(H)
. This follows directly from Proposition
4.29
. We obtain four elements
\langle g,h\rangle,\langle g,\widehat{h}\rangle,\langle\widehat{g},h\rangle,\langle\widehat{g},\widehat{h}\rangle
inside
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
. But the parameter space
\mathcal{S}
produces
|\{R_{st},R_{ex}\}|^{3}=2^{3}
different maps. If we don’t put the equivalence relation we would be over-counting the number of representations, that is we would construct
\widetilde{\rho},\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}\in\chi^{\text{max}}(W_{3},{\rm PSp}^{\pm}(4,\mathbb{R}))
that have the same image under
f
.

From representations to parameters
: This is as Theorem
7.16
.

∎

Corollary 7.19
.

The map
f
of Definition
7.15
is neither injective nor surjective.

Proof.

It is clear that
f
is not injective. We show
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}\subsetneq\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
. The space
\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
is 10-dimensional (see for example
[
AGRW19
]
). In the parametrization of Theorem
7.18
we see that the set
\mathcal{S}
is 8-dimensional. To see this observe that the space of right-angled hexagons is 8 dimensional. In the construction of Theorem
7.18
if the hexagon is generic then the
\mathcal{K}
-component is 0-dimensional, and whenever one length-parameter lands in
\mathfrak{d}
(the hexagon becomes non-generic) then the
\mathcal{K}
-component is one-dimensional (it is
{\rm PO}(2)
).
∎

7.8.
Connected components

The parameter space of
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
distinguishes 8 connected components. The space
\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
has
4
connected components (see
[
AGRW19
]
). Let us see this by studying the geometrical behaviour of the generators of the representations.

For a
g
in
{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R})
which fixes two transverse Lagrangians
l_{1},l_{2}
there is a natural way to associate to
g
a number
sgn(g)\in\{\pm 1\}
depending on
g
being a reflecting or a non-reflecting isometry as in Definition
2.30
. More precisely
sgn(g)=1
if
\det A>0
where
g\sim\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&A^{-T}\end{pmatrix}
and
sgn(g)=-1
if
\det A<0
. The number
sgn(g)
tells us whether or not
g
is reversing the orientation of the hyperbolic component of the tube
\mathcal{Y}_{l_{1},l_{2}}
. Given
\Gamma_{0,3}=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
, we consider a continuous surjective map
\delta
which distinghuishes the connected components of
\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
:

(23)

\displaystyle\delta:\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))

\displaystyle\to\{\pm 1\}\times\{\pm 1\}

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(sgn(\rho(\alpha)),sgn(\rho(\beta))\big)

Lemma 7.20
.

Let
\delta
be the map in (
23
). Then for a point of parameters
(p,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
inside
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
it holds
\delta(p,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})=\big(sgn(R_{1}R_{2}),sgn(R_{2}R_{3})\big)
.

Proof.

In the proof of Theorem
7.18
we have explicitly constructed the representation
\rho\in\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
from the parameters
(p,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\in\mathcal{S}
. The generators of the representation are given by
\overline{R}_{1}\overline{R}_{2}
and
\overline{R}_{2}\overline{R}_{3}
, where

\overline{R}_{1}:=g_{1}^{-1}R_{1}g_{1}
,

\overline{R}_{2}:=g_{2}^{-1}R_{2}g_{2}
, and
\overline{R}_{3}:=g_{3}^{-1}R_{3}g_{3}
for a suitable choice of
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}
(see proof of Theorem
7.16
).
We need to prove
sgn(\overline{R}_{i}\overline{R}_{j})=sgn(R_{i}R_{j})
. Let us concentrate on the case
i=1,j=2
. The geometrical behaviour of
\overline{R}_{1}\overline{R}_{2}
is shown in Figure
33
in the case where
p
determines a generic hexagon. The property of reflecting the hyperbolic component (i.e. being a reflecting isometry as in Definition
2.30
) or not only depends on
sgn(R_{1}R_{2})
. Sending a polygonal chain with internal angles
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
to one with same internal angles implies being a non-reflecting isometry. This can be generalized for any
i,j
.
∎

Proposition 7.21
.

The set
\chi^{\mathcal{S}}
hits all connected component inside
\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
.

Proof.

This is clear. In particular each of the four connected components of
\chi^{\text{max,Shilov}}(\Gamma_{0,3},{\rm PSp}(4,\mathbb{R}))
contains two connected components of
\chi^{s}
.
∎
